Node Replication: Theory and Practice
Delia Kesner, Loïc Peyrot, Daniel Ventura

Abstract
. We define and study a term calculus implementing higher-order node replication. It is used to specify two different (weak) evaluation strategies: call-by-name and fully lazy call-by-need, that are shown to be observationally equivalent by using type theoretical technical tools.



Jul. 18, 2022Jan. 23, 2024

[a,c]
[a]
[b]

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 20, Issue 1, Paper 5

1.
Introduction

Computation in the
\lambda
-calculus is based on higher-order
substitution, a complex operation being able to erase and copy terms
during evaluation. Several formalisms have been proposed to model
higher-order substitution, going from explicit substitutions
(ES)
[
ACCL90
]
(see a survey in
[
Kes09
]
) and labeled
systems
[
Lév78
,
BLM05
]
to pointer graphs
[
Wad71
]
or
optimal sharing graphs
[
Lam90
]
. The operational
semantics used in each formalism to implement copying
(
i.e.
duplication) of terms is not the same.

Meanwhile, the Curry-Howard isomorphism
[
SU06
]
uncovers a
deep connection between logical systems and term calculi. Also in this
framework, different ways to normalize proofs in different logical
systems correspond to different implementations of substitution.
Indeed, the process of
full substitution
is somehow
analogous to the normalization process in natural deduction, while

linear substitution
corresponds to cut elimination in
Proof-Nets
[
Acc18a
]
.
Node-by-node replication
is
based on a Curry-Howard interpretation of deep
inference
[
GGP10
,
GHP13a
]
. Let us illustrate these
different models using an example.

Indeed, suppose one wants to substitute all the free occurrences of some
variable
x
in a term
t=x\!\cdot\!x
by the term
u=y\!\cdot\!(z\!\cdot\!w)
(
\_\!\cdot\!\_
denotes the
application constructor).
The variable substituted at each reduction step is going to be highlighted in
light blue
:

\displaystyle(\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$}\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$})[x/u]

\displaystyle\rightarrow u\!\cdot\!u

(1)

\displaystyle(\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$}\!\cdot\!x)[x/u]

\displaystyle\rightarrow(u\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$})[x/u]\rightarrow u\!\cdot\!u

(2)

\displaystyle(\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$}\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$})[x/y\!\cdot\!(w\!\cdot\!z)]

\displaystyle\rightarrow((y\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x^{\prime}$})\!\cdot\!(y\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x^{\prime}$}))[x^{\prime}/w\!\cdot\!z]\rightarrow u\!\cdot\!u

(3)

Full (or
non-linear
) substitution (
1
) is a
one-shot substitution, replacing simultaneously
all
the free occurrences of
x
in
t
by the whole term
u
.
This notion is generally defined by induction on the structure of the term
t
.
Linear (or
partial
)
substitution (
2
) replaces
one
free occurrence of
x
at a time. This
notion is generally defined by induction on the number of free occurrences of

x
in the term
t
.
Node replication (
3
) is yet another approach
which replicates
one
term-constructor of
u

at a time
, instead of replicating
u
as a whole.
This notion can be defined by
induction on the structure of the term
u
.
In the Curry-Howard interpretation of deep inference, node replication is full:
all occurences of
x
are replaced by a node of
u
. A
linear version of the node replication approach can be formally defined by
combining the last two models, although this does not
correspond to any logical system we are aware of:

\displaystyle(\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$}\!\cdot\!x)[x/u]

\displaystyle\rightarrow((y\!\cdot\!x^{\prime})\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x$})[x/y\!\cdot\!x^{\prime}][x^{\prime}/w\!\cdot\!z]\rightarrow((y\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x^{\prime}$})\!\cdot\!(y\!\cdot\!x^{\prime}))[x^{\prime}/w\!\cdot\!z]

(4)

\displaystyle\rightarrow((y\!\cdot\!(w\!\cdot\!z))\!\cdot\!(y\!\cdot\!\hbox{\pagecolor{celeste}$x^{\prime}$}))[x^{\prime}/w\!\cdot\!z]\rightarrow(y\!\cdot\!(w\!\cdot\!z))\!\cdot\!(y\!\cdot\!(w\!\cdot\!z))=u\!\cdot\!u

In this sense, node
replication is orthogonal to the full/linear aspect of

classical
substitution, since it can be
implemented by either full or linear operations, as explained above.
Unsurprisingly, different notions of
substitution give rise to different evaluation strategies.
Indeed, linear substitution is the common model in
well-known abstract machines for call-by-name and
call-by-value (see
e.g.

[
ABM14
]
), while (linear) node replication
can be used to implement fully
lazy sharing
[
Wad71
]
. However, node replication, originally
introduced to implement optimal graph
reduction in a graphical formalism, has only
been studied from a Curry-Howard perspective by means of a term
language known as the atomic

\lambda
-calculus
[
GHP13a
]
.

The Atomic Lambda-Calculus.

Logical aspects of intuistionistic deep inference are captured by the
atomic
\lambda
-calculus
\lambda a

[
GHP13a
]
, where copying
of terms proceeds
atomically
,
i.e.
node by node, similar to the
optimal graph reduction of Lamping
[
Lam90
]
. The atomic

\lambda
-calculus is based on
explicit control of resources
,
such as erasure and duplication. Its operational semantics explicitly
handles the structural constructors of weakening and contraction, as
in the calculus of resources
\lambda{\tt lxr}

[
KL07
,
KR11
]
. As a result,
understanding the meta-properties of the
term-calculus, in a higher-level, and its application to concrete
implementations of reduction strategies in programming languages, turn
out to be quite difficult. In this paper, we take one step back, by
studying the paradigm of
node replication
based on

implicit
, rather than
explicit
, weakening and
contraction. This gives a new concise formulation of node replication
which is simple enough to model different programming languages based
on reduction strategies.

Call-by-Name, Call-by-Value, Call-by-Need.

The theory of programming is usually based on some notion of

calculus
, which can be often seen as a higher-order rewriting
system. A calculus
definition results in general in a
non-deterministic and unrestricted relation: a term can be reduced in
different ways, some of them being more efficent than others. When
implementing programming languages based on these theories, a
specific (deterministic)
reduction strategy
is necessary to provide a
concrete mechanism to evaluate a program.

In the specific case of functional programming, the theory is given by the
\lambda
-calculus, giving rise to different reduction strategies used by different functional programming languages.

Call-by-name
is used to implement programming languages in which
arguments of functions are first copied, then evaluated. This is frequently
expensive, and may be improved by
call-by-value
, in which arguments are
evaluated first, then consumed.
The difference can be illustrated by the term
t=\Delta(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
, where

\Delta=\lambda x.xx
and
\mathtt{I}=\lambda z.z
: call-by-name first duplicates the
argument
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
, so that its evaluation is also duplicated, while
call-by-value first reduces
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
to (the value)
\mathtt{I}
, so that duplications
of the argument do not cause any duplicated evaluation. It is not always the
best solution, though, because evaluating erasable arguments is useless.
Compare for instance
(\lambda x.z)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\rightarrow_{\beta}(\lambda x.z)\mathtt{I}\rightarrow_{\beta}z
to
(\lambda x.z)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\rightarrow_{\beta}z
.

Call-by-need
, instead, combines the best of call-by-name and
call-by-value: as in call-by-name, erasable arguments are not evaluated at all,
and as in call-by-value, reduction of arguments occurs at most once.
Furthermore, call-by-need implements a

demand-driven
evaluation, in which erasable arguments are never
needed (so they are not evaluated), and non-erasable arguments are
evaluated only if needed. Technically, some sharing mechanism is
necessary, for example by extending the
\lambda
-calculus with explicit
substitutions/let constructs
[
AF97
]
. Then, any
\beta
-reduction
step can be
decomposed in at least two steps: one creating an explicit (pending)
substitution, and the other ones (linearly)
substituting
values
. Thus for example,

(\lambda x.xx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
reduces to
(xx)[x/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]
,
and the substitution argument is thus evaluated in order to find a value before
performing the linear substitution.

Even when adopting this wise evaluation scheme, there are still some
unnecessary copies of redexes: while only
values

(
i.e.
abstractions) are duplicated, they may contain redexes as subterms,

e.g.

\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
whose
subterm
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
is a redex. Duplication of such values
might cause redex duplications in
weak
(
i.e.
when evaluation is forbidden inside abstractions) call-by-need.
This happens in particular in the
confluent
variant of weak reduction in
[
LM99
]
:

\begin{array}[]{llllll}(\lambda x.xx)(\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}))&\rightarrow&(xx)[x/\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})]&\rightarrow&((\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}))x)[x/\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})]&\\
&\rightarrow&(z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}))[z/x][x/\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})]&\rightarrow&(x(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}))[x/\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})]&\\
&\twoheadrightarrow&{(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})}&\twoheadrightarrow&\mathtt{I}&\\
\end{array}

Full laziness.

Alas, it is not possible to keep all values shared forever,
typically when they potentially contribute to the creation of a future

\beta
-reduction step. The key idea to gain efficiency is then
to keep the subterm
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
as a
shared
redex.
Therefore, the value
\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})

to be copied is split into two separate parts. The first one, called

skeleton
, contains the minimal information
preserving the
bound structure of the value,
i.e.
the linked structure between the binder and
each of its bound variables. In our example, this is the term
\lambda z.zy
, where
y
is a fresh variable. The second one is a multiset
of
maximal free expressions
(MFE), representing all the
shareable expressions (here only the term
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
).
Only the skeleton is then copied, while
the problematic redex
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
remains shared:

(\lambda x.xx)(\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}))\rightarrow(xx)[x/\lambda z.z(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})]\rightarrow((\lambda z.zy)x)[x/\lambda z.zy][y/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]

When the subterm
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
is
needed ahead, it is first reduced inside the explicit substitution,
as it is usual in call-by-need, thus
avoiding to compute the redex twice.
This optimization is called
fully lazy sharing

and is due to Wadsworth
[
Wad71
]
.

As shown by Balabonski
[
Bal13
]
, any weak strategy
of the
\lambda
-calculus which is not using sharing is undecidable,
as for example the
innermost needed reduction
. However, in
the confluent weak setting evoked earlier
[
LM99
]
, the fully
lazy optimization is even optimal in the sense of
Lévy
[
Lév80
]
. This means that the strategy reaches the weak
normal form in the same number of
\beta
-steps as the shortest
possible weak reduction sequence in the usual
\lambda
-calculus
without sharing. Thus, fully lazy sharing turns out to be a

decidable
optimal strategy

Quantitative Types

Intersection types were introduced as
(denotational)

models capturing computational properties of functional
programming in a broader
sense
[
CD78
,
CDCV81
,
BCDC83
]
.
They extend simple types with a new
constructor
\cap
, thus allowing to support
polymorphism in a finitary way: a
program
t
is typable with
\sigma\cap\tau
if
t
is typable
with both types
\sigma
and
\tau
independently. Originally,
intersection enjoys associativity, commutativity, and in particular
idempotency (
i.e.

\sigma\cap\sigma=\sigma
). By changing to a

non-idempotent
intersection constructor, one naturally comes
to represent such a type by a
multiset, also known as

multi-type
. Idempotent as well as
non-idempotent types allow for a characterization of several
operational properties of programs,
e.g.
termination
of different evaluations strategies can be characterized by
typability in some appropriate intersection type system. There
is however a major difference between the two: while idempotent
types provide
qualitative
information, non-idempotent
ones also provide
quantitative
knowledge.
More precisely, it is not only possible to prove
that typability in one typing system characterizes
termination of some particular evaluation strategy,
i.e.
that a
program is terminating if and only if it is typable in the corresponding system, but also an
upper bound
or
exact measure
for the time needed for its
evaluation can be derived from its typing information
(see
[
BKV17
]
for a survey).

Contributions.

The first contribution of this paper is a term
calculus implementing (full) node replication (
Section 2
) and internally
encoding skeleton extraction (
Subsection 4.2
). We study some of
its main operational properties: termination of the
substitution calculus, confluence, and its relation with the
\lambda
-calculus.

Our second contribution is the use of the node replication paradigm to
give an alternative specification of two evaluation
strategies usually described by means of full or linear substitution:
call-by-name (
Subsection 4.1
) and weak fully lazy reduction
(
Subsection 4.2
), based on the key notion of skeleton.
The former can be related to (weak) head
reduction, while the latter is a fully lazy version of (weak)
call-by-need. In contrast to other implementations of fully lazy
reduction relying on (external) meta-level definitions, our
implementation is based on formal operations internally defined over
the syntax of the calculus.

Our implementation of fully lazy reduction is based on
a class of
restricted
terms, called
U
,
which simplifies the formal reasoning. This restriction is not ad-hoc in the sense it is stable through evaluations based on weak strategies, i.e. is an invariant property of evaluations starting from pure
\lambda
-terms.

Furthermore, while it is known that call-by-name and call-by-need
specified by means of full/linear substitution are observationally
equivalent
[
AF97
]
, it was not clear at first whether the same
property would hold in our case. Our third contribution is a proof of this
result (
Section 6
) using semantical tools coming
from proof theory –notably intersection types. This proof
technique
[
Kes16
]
considerably simplifies other
approaches
[
AF97
,
MOW98
]
based on syntactical
tools. Moreover, the use of intersection types has another important consequence:
standard call-by-name and call-by-need turn out to be
observationally equivalent to call-by-name and call-by-need
with node replication, as
well as to the more semantical notion of neededness (see
[
KRV18
]
).

Intersection types provide
quantitative information about fully lazy evaluation so that a fourth
contribution of this work is a measure based on type
derivations which turns out to be an upper bound to the
length of reduction sequences to normal forms in a fully lazy
implementation.

More generally, our work bridges the gap between the Curry-Howard
theoretical understanding of node replication and concrete
implementations of fully lazy sharing.
Related works are presented in the concluding
Section 7
.

2.
A Calculus for Node Replication

We now present the
\lambda R
-calculus (as in
\mathtt{R}
eplication).
From a syntactical point of view, we add two new constructors to the

\lambda
-calculus: explicit substitutions and explicit distributors. From
an operational point of view, we provide a rewriting
system on
\lambda R
-terms together with a notion of
levels

which will play a key role in the next sections.

2.1.
Syntax

Given a countably infinite set of variables
x,y,z,...
, we consider the following grammars.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{-.5mm}}rcll}\mbox{({Terms})}\hfil\hskip-1.42262pt&t,u,r,s&\Coloneqq&x\mid\lambda x.t\mid tu\mid t[x/u]\mid t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]\\
\mbox{({Pure Terms})}\hfil\hskip-1.42262pt&p,q&\Coloneqq&x\mid\lambda x.p\mid pq\\
\mbox{({Term Contexts})}\hfil\hskip-1.42262pt&\mathtt{C}&\Coloneqq&\Diamond\mid\lambda x.\mathtt{C}\mid\mathtt{C}t\mid t\mathtt{C}\mid\mathtt{C}[x/t]\mid\mathtt{C}[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]\mid t[x/\mathtt{C}]\mid t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.\mathtt{C}]\\
\mbox{({List Contexts})}\hfil\hskip-1.42262pt&\mathtt{L}&\Coloneqq&\Diamond\mid\mathtt{L}[x/u]\mid\mathtt{L}[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]\end{array}

The set of terms is denoted by
\mathtt{T}_{R}
and the subset of
pure
terms is
denoted by
\mathtt{T}_{P}
.
We write
\mathtt{I}
for the identity function
\lambda x.x
, and
|t|
for the number of symbols of the term
t
.

The construction
[x/u]
is an
explicit substitution
, which
allows the
sharing
of the subterm
u
. A term
t[x/u]
can be simply seen as a more concise notation
for a
let-binding

{{{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{let}}}}}}}\ x\ \text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor=}}}}\ u\ \text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{in}}}}}}}\ t
, where
u
is shared across the
free occurences of the variable
x
in
t
. The second construction

[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]
is an
explicit distributor
(or
simply
distributor
), which is used specifically in the
duplication of abstractions.

An
explicit cut
is denoted by
[x\triangleleft u]
, which is either
[x/u]
, or

[x/\!\!/u]
when
u
is
\lambda y.u^{\prime}
, typically to factorize some definitions and
proofs where explicit substitutions and distributors behave similarly.
A characterization function
\operatorname{ES}(\cdot)
on explicit cuts distinguishes these two cases:
\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])=1
if
[x\triangleleft u]=[x/u]
, and
0
otherwise.
The application constructor associates to the left,
i.e.

t_{1}t_{2}\ldots t_{n}
means
((t_{1}t_{2})\ldots t_{n})
. Explicit cuts affects the rightmost constructor,
i.e.

tu[x\triangleleft s]
means
t(u[x\triangleleft s])
, otherwise we will write parenthesis like
(tu)[x\triangleleft s]
.

Two notions of
contexts
are used. Term contexts
\mathtt{C}
extend
those of the
\lambda
-calculus to explicit cuts. A term context is said to be
pure
if it does not contain any explicit cut. List contexts
\mathtt{L}
denote an
arbitrary list of explicit cuts. They will be used in particular to
implement reduction at a
distance
(see
Subsection 2.2
).

Free
and
bound variables
of terms are defined as
expected, in particular
\operatorname{fv}(\lambda x.t)\coloneqq\operatorname{fv}(t)\backslash\{x\}

and
\operatorname{fv}(t[x\triangleleft u])\coloneqq\operatorname{fv}(t)\backslash\{x\}\cup\operatorname{fv}(u)
.
We write
|t|_{x}
to denote the number of free occurrences of
the variable
x
in the term
t
.
These notions are extended
to contexts as expected. In particular
\operatorname{fv}(\Diamond)\coloneqq\emptyset

and
\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{L}[x\triangleleft u])\coloneqq\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{L})\backslash\{x\}\cup\operatorname{fv}(u)
.
The
domain
of a list context is defined as

\operatorname{dom}(\Diamond)\coloneqq\emptyset

and
\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{L}[x\triangleleft u])\coloneqq\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{L})\cup\{x\}
.

The standard notion of
\alpha
-conversion

[
Bar85
]
on

\lambda
-terms is extended to
the full set of
\lambda R
-terms as expected,
and we systematically assume
\alpha
-conversion
whenever necessary to avoid capture of free
variables. We write
t\{x/u\}
for the
meta-level
substitution
operation simultaneously replacing all
the free occurrences of the variable
x
in
t
by the term
u
.

The
application of a context

\mathtt{C}
to a term
t
, written
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
, replaces the hole
\Diamond
of
\mathtt{C}
by
t
. Thus, for
instance,
\Diamond\langle t\rangle=t
and
(\lambda x.\Diamond)\langle t\rangle=\lambda x.t
.
This operation is not defined modulo

\alpha
-conversion, so that capture of variables eventually
happens, such as in the second example if
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
. Thus,
another kind of application of contexts to
terms is also considered, identified by double brackets, and is only defined if
there is no capture of variables. For
instance,

(\lambda y.\Diamond)\langle x\rangle=\lambda y.x
while
(\lambda x.\Diamond)\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
is undefined.

2.2.
Operational semantics

Before presenting the dynamics of the calculus, let us introduce some notations
and definitions concerning reduction.
Let
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
be any reduction
relation. We write
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
when
(t,t^{\prime})\in\;\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
, and
\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}

(resp.
\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}
) for the reflexive-transitive (resp. transitive) closure
of
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. A term
t
is said to be
\mathcal{R}
-confluent

iff
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}s
implies there is
t^{\prime}

such that
u\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
and
s\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
. The relation

\mathcal{R}
is
confluent
iff every term is
\mathcal{R}
-confluent.
A term
t
is said to be in
\mathcal{R}
-normal form
(written also

\mathcal{R}
-nf) iff there is no
t^{\prime}
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
. A
term
t
is said to be
\mathcal{R}
-terminating
or

\mathcal{R}
-normalizing
iff there is no infinite

\mathcal{R}
-sequence starting at
t
. The reduction
\mathcal{R}
is said
to be
terminating
iff every term is
\mathcal{R}
-terminating.

Explicit substitutions may block some expected
meaningful

(
i.e.
non-structural) reductions. For instance,
\beta
-reduction is
blocked in
(\lambda x.t)[y/s]u
because an explicit substitution
lies between the function and its argument. This situation does not happen in graphical representations
(
e.g.

[
Gir96
]
), but it is typical in the sequential structure
of
term
syntaxes.

There are at least two ways to handle this issue. The first one
is based on
structural/permutation
rules, as
in
[
GHP13a
]
. Therefore, in the previous example, the
substitution is first pushed outside the application node by means of
a permutation rule, as
(\lambda x.t)[y/s]u\rightarrow((\lambda x.t)u)[y/s]
,
so that
\beta
-reduction is finally unblocked. The second,
less elementary, possibility is given by an operational semantics

at a distance

[
AK10
,
ABKL14
]
, where the

\beta
-redex can be fired by a rule like
\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda x.t\rangle u\rightarrow\mathtt{L}\langle t[x/u]\rangle
, where
\mathtt{L}
is an arbitrary list
context. The distant paradigm is therefore used to gather meaningful
and permutation rules in only one reduction step. In
\lambda R
, we
combine these two complementary technical tools. First, we consider
the following permutation rules:

\begin{array}[]{llll}\lambda y.t[x\triangleleft u]&\mapsto_{\pi}&(\lambda y.t)[x\triangleleft u]&\text{ if }y\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)\\
t[x\triangleleft u]s&\mapsto_{\pi}&(ts)[x\triangleleft u]&\text{ if }x\notin\operatorname{fv}(s)\\
ts[x\triangleleft u]&\mapsto_{\pi}&(ts)[x\triangleleft u]&\text{ if }x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)\\
t[x\triangleleft u[y\triangleleft s]]&\mapsto_{\pi}&t[x\triangleleft u][y\triangleleft s]&\text{ if }y\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)\end{array}

The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\pi}
is defined as the closure of the four
rules
\mapsto_{\pi}
under
all
contexts.

{exa}

Let
t=x[x/w[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}](\lambda y.y[z_{2}/z_{3}])]
.
Both inner explict cuts
[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}]
and
[z_{2}/z_{3}]
are pushed
outside the main explicit substitution, which results in a pure term followed
by a list of explicit cuts.

\begin{array}[]{llllll}t&\rightarrow_{\pi}&x[x/w[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}](\lambda y.y)[z_{2}/z_{3}]]&&\rightarrow_{\pi}&x[x/(w[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}](\lambda y.y))[z_{2}/z_{3}]]\\
&\rightarrow_{\pi}&x[x/w[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}](\lambda y.y)][z_{2}/z_{3}]&&\rightarrow_{\pi}&x[x/(w(\lambda y.y))[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}]][z_{2}/z_{3}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\pi}&x[x/w(\lambda y.y)][z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}][z_{2}/z_{3}]\end{array}

Permutations do not hold any computational content, only a structural one. Indeed, all terms in the
reduction sequence above could be naturally translated
to the same graphical notation. In order to highlight the
computational content of node replication, we combine distance and
permutations within a single rewriting semantics.
In addition, because operational semantics at a distance is
directly inspired from graph formalisms, there is a better correspondence between the syntactic representation of terms and the graphical
representations of their associated reduction
notion
[
KL07
,
Acc18b
]
.
The resulting reduction relation

\lambda R
is given by
the closure under all contexts of the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle\lambda x.t\rangle u&\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}&\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle t[x/u]\rangle\\
t[x/\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle us\rangle]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}}&\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]\rangle&\mbox{where $y$ and $z$ are fresh}\\
t[x/\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle\lambda y.u\rangle]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}}&\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]]\rangle&\mbox{where $z$ is fresh}\\
t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}&\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle t\{x/\lambda y.p\}\rangle&\mbox{where }u\twoheadrightarrow_{\pi}\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle p\rangle\mbox{ and }y\notin\operatorname{fv}(\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$})\\
t[x/\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle y\rangle]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}}&\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$\mathtt{L}$}\langle t\{x/y\}\rangle\\
\end{array}

where the distant contexts are highlighted in
green
to make it easier to read.
The
\lambda R
-calculus
is defined by the set of terms
\mathtt{T}_{R}
equipped
with this reduction relation.
In the five rules just above, a list context
\mathtt{L}
is pushed outside
the term. We assume in all these cases that there is no capture of
variables caused by this transformation,
e.g.
in rule
\mathtt{dB}
this means
that
\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{L})\cap\operatorname{fv}(u)=\emptyset
. Apart from the
distant

{\mathtt{B}}
eta
rule
\mathtt{dB}
used to fire
\beta
-reduction, there are
four substitution rules used to copy nodes of
pure
terms
pushing outside all the cuts surrounding the node to be copied.
Rule
\mathtt{app}
copies one application node, while
rule
\mathtt{var}
copies one variable node.
To copy abstractions, both rules
\mathtt{dist}
and
\mathtt{abs}
are needed.
Notice that the (meta-level and capture-free) substitution is
full
, in the sense that
it is performed simultaneously on all ocurrences of the free variable
x
at the same time.

The reduction relation
\mapsto_{\mathtt{sub}}
is defined as

\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}}\cup\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}}\cup\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}\cup\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}}
, while the
\mathtt{s}
ubstitution relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
(resp. distant Beta relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}
) is defined as the
closure of
\mapsto_{\mathtt{sub}}
(resp.
\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}
) under
all

contexts, and the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}
is the union of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}
.

{exa}

This example illustrates the use of rules
\mathtt{app}
and
\mathtt{var}
to
replicate application and variables nodes,
as well as rule
\mathtt{dB}
to fire reduction.
No distance is involved in this example.

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\lambda x.xx)(yz)&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}(xx)[x/yz]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}((x_{1}x_{2})(x_{1}x_{2}))[x_{1}/y][x_{2}/z]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(yx_{2})(yx_{2})[x_{2}/z]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(yz)(yz)\end{array}

{exa}

Replication of abstractions is more involved, as illustrated below.

\displaystyle(\lambda x.xx)(\lambda y.(ww)y)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}(xx)[x/\lambda y.(ww)y]

(5)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}(xx)[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/(ww)y]]

(6)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}(xx)[x/\!\!/\lambda y.(z_{1}z_{2})[z_{1}/ww][z_{2}/y]]

(7)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(xx)[x/\!\!/\lambda y.(z_{1}y)[z_{1}/ww]]

(8)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}(xx)[x/\!\!/\lambda y.((z_{3}z_{2})y)\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[z_{3}/w][z_{2}/w]$}]

(9)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}((\lambda y.(z_{3}z_{2})y)(\lambda y.(z_{3}z_{2})y))[z_{3}/w][z_{2}/w]

(10)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}((\lambda y.(wz_{2})y)(\lambda y.(wz_{2})y))[z_{2}/w]

(11)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(\lambda y.(ww)y)(\lambda y.(ww)y)

(12)

The specificity in copying an abstraction
\lambda y.u
is due to the
(binding) relation between the binder
\lambda y
and all the free
occurrences of
y
in its body
u
. Abstractions are thus copied in
two stages. The first one is implemented by the rule
\mathtt{dist}
, which
creates a distributor in which a potentially replicable abstraction is
placed, while moving its body inside a new explicit substitution.
Thus, in line (
6
), we create a distributor
over the abstraction
\lambda y
, while
(ww)y
is placed inside an explicit
substitution
[z/(ww)y]
. Notice that this substitution is in
the scope of abstraction
\lambda y
. The distributor is marking the
fact that the abstraction needs to be further duplicated.
There are then two kinds of potentially replicable nodes shared in the body of
the corresponding abstraction.

(1)

All free occurences of the
variable bound by the main abstraction (here
\lambda y
)
must be replicated by means of the rule
\mathtt{var}
(
8
),
so as to keep the correct binding structure.
This means that all the nodes leading to these
occurences must also be duplicated:
this is why rule
\mathtt{app}
is first used in (
7
).

(2)

All nodes which are neither a free
occurence of the bound variable nor in the path to
such a node can be arbitrarily copied inside the distributor
(
e.g.
the internal application node in line (
9
)),
or replicated later (
e.g.
the two variable nodes
w

in (
11
) and (
12
)).

Components which are not replicated inside the
distributor form a list of explicit cuts, which can occur at
different depths inside this distributor. Indeed, in (
9
),
there are two explicit substitutions
[z_{3}/w]
and
[z_{2}/w]
.
The cuts can be
gathered together into a list context, called
\mathtt{L}
in the definition
of rule
\mathtt{abs}
, which is pushed outside by using
permutation rules, before performing the substitution of the pure
body containing all the bound occurrences of
y
(here
\lambda y.(z_{1}z_{2})y
).
This operation is in general hard to specify using only distance since the cuts can
appear at arbitrary depth in the distributor, and this
is one of the reasons to introduce the use of permutation rules in
rule
\mathtt{abs}
.

Other choices are possible, such as replicating all the nodes, or only the
uppermost application and the node
y
(corresponding to fully lazy
duplication), as long as at least all free occurences of
y
are duplicated.

{exa}

This last example showcases different reduction steps with distance,
highlighted in green.

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x)\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[z_{4}/z_{5}]$}(w[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}](\lambda y.y[z_{2}/z_{3}]))

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}x[x/w[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}](\lambda y.y[z_{2}/z_{3}])][z_{4}/z_{5}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}(x_{1}x_{2})[x_{1}/w\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}]$}][x_{2}/\lambda y.y[z_{2}/z_{3}]][z_{4}/z_{5}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(wx_{2})[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}][x_{2}/\lambda y.y[z_{2}/z_{3}]][z_{4}/z_{5}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}(wx_{2})[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}][x_{2}/\!\!/\lambda y.x[x/y\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[z_{2}/z_{3}]$}]][z_{4}/z_{5}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(wx_{2})[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}][x_{2}/\!\!/\lambda y.y\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[z_{2}/z_{3}]$}][z_{4}/z_{5}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}(w(\lambda y.y))[z_{1}/\!\!/\mathtt{I}][z_{2}/z_{3}][z_{4}/z_{5}]

Notice that an
\mathtt{R}
-step can be decomposed into some

\pi
-steps followed by a simpler step not involving any
list context. Indeed,
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}u
could be simulated by
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\pi}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}^{\prime}}u
, where
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}^{\prime}}
is the closure under
all contexts of the following set of rewriting rules:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(\lambda x.t)u&\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}^{\prime}}&t[x/u]&\\
t[x/us]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}^{\prime}}&t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]&\\
t[x/\lambda y.u]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}^{\prime}}&t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]]&\\
t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.p]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}^{\prime}}&t\{x/\lambda y.p\}\\
t[x/y]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}^{\prime}}&t\{x/y\}&\\
\end{array}

For instance, step (
10
) in
Subsection 2.2
can be decomposed
as follows, where
r=\lambda y.(z_{3}z_{2})y
:

(xx)[x/\!\!/r[z_{3}/w][z_{2}/w]]\twoheadrightarrow_{\pi}(xx)[x/\!\!/r][z_{3}/w][z_{2}/w]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}^{\prime}}(rr)[z_{3}/w][z_{2}/w]

This decomposition will be useful in some of our proofs, but we prefer to integrate
distance inside the rules, as initially defined on
page
2.2
, to highlight the computational behavior and
execute permutations only when strictly necessary.

2.3.
Levels

This subsection introduces the syntactical notion of level and its
associated properties. Intuitively, the level of a variable in a term
indicates the maximal depth (
only
w.r.t. explicit substitutions
and not w.r.t. explicit distributors) of its free
occurrences. However, in order to be sound with respect to
the permutation rules, levels do not consider depth in the
usual sense only, but also across linked chains of explicit
substitutions. For instance, the level of
z
in both

(xx)[x/y[y/z]]

and
(xx)[x/y][y/z]

is the same.
Levels will play a key role in the next sections:
they will be the combinatorial witnesses of
the progress of
\mathtt{sub}
-substitution steps, necessary to prove
termination of the
\mathtt{sub}
-relation.
They will also be helpful to define a decreasing measure on typing derivations
in
Section 5
.
The
level
of a variable
z
in a term
t
is defined by induction:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}t_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}))

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(\lambda x.t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t[x\triangleleft u])

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)&\text{if }z\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)\\
\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

In the two last cases, we can always suppose
z\neq x
, because we work modulo

\alpha
-conversion.
Notice that
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)=0
whenever
z\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
or
t
is pure.
We illustrate the concept of level by an example.
Consider
t=x[x/z[y/w]][w/w^{\prime}]
,
then
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)=1
,
\operatorname{lv}_{w^{\prime}}(t)=3
and
\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)=0
because
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
.
This notion is also extended to contexts as expected,
i.e.

\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{C})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(\mathtt{C}\langle z\rangle)
, where
z
is a fresh variable.
Remark that for any variable
x
,
\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{C})\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)

and
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle)\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)
for any
p\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
.

{restatable}

lemlevelsubstitution
Let
x\neq z
,
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
and
p\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
:

(1)

If
z\notin\operatorname{fv}(p)
, then
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)
.

(2)

If
z\in\operatorname{fv}(p)
,
then
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:level-substitution).
∎

{restatable}

lemstabilitylevels
Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
and
w
be any variable.

(1)

If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\pi}t_{1}
,
then
\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t_{0})\geq\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t_{1})
.

(2)

If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
, then
\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t_{0})\geq\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t_{1})
.

Proof.

By induction on the reduction relation
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:stability-levels).
∎

Notice that there are two cases when the level of a variable in a term may decrease:

•

Moving an explicit cut out of another one
with a permutation rule when the
first cut is a void cut,
i.e.
its domain does not bind any other variable.
Thus
e.g.
if
t=x[x/z[y/w]][w/w^{\prime}]\rightarrow_{\pi}x[x/z][y/w][w/w^{\prime}]=u
,
then
\operatorname{lv}_{w^{\prime}}(t)=3>2=\operatorname{lv}_{w^{\prime}}(u)
.

•

Using rule
\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}}
.
Thus
e.g.
if
t=(xx)[x/y][y/z]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(yy)[y/z]=u
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)=2>1=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(u)
.

Hence, levels alone are not enough
to prove termination of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
. We thus define a decreasing measure for

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
in which not only variables are indexed by a
level, but also constructors. For instance, in the term
t[x/\lambda y.yz]
,
we can consider that the level of

all
the constructors of
\lambda y.yz
,
including the abstraction and the application, have level
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)
.
This will ensure that the level of an abstraction will
decrease when applying rule
\mathtt{dist}
, as well as the level of an
application when applying rule
\mathtt{app}
.

3.
Operational Properties

We now prove three key properties of the

\lambda R
-calculus: termination of the
reduction system
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
,
relation between
\lambda R
and the
\lambda
-calculus, and confluence of the
reduction system
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}
.

Termination of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}

Some (rather informal) arguments are provided in
[
GHP13a
]
to justify
termination of the substitution subrelation of their calculus.
We expand these ideas into an alternative full formal proof adapted to our case,
which is based on a measure being strictly decreasing w.r.t.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
.

We consider a set
\mathcal{O}
of objects of the form
\subesname(k,n)
or

\subdistname(k)\ (k,n\in\mathbb{N})
,
which is equipped with the following ordering
>^{\mathcal{O}}

(
\geq^{\mathcal{O}}
denotes its reflexive closure):

\begin{array}[]{rll@{\hspace{.25cm}}rll}\subesname(k,n)&>^{\mathcal{O}}\subesname(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})&\mbox{if }k>k^{\prime},\mbox{ or }(k=k^{\prime}\mbox{ and }n>n^{\prime})\hfil\hskip 7.11317pt&\subdistname(k)&>^{\mathcal{O}}\subesname(k^{\prime},n)&\mbox{if }k\geq k^{\prime}\\
\subesname(k,n)&>^{\mathcal{O}}\subdistname(k^{\prime})&\mbox{if }k>k^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 7.11317pt&\subdistname(k)&>^{\mathcal{O}}\subdistname(k^{\prime})&\mbox{if }k>k^{\prime}\end{array}

Notice that the symbols
\subesname
and
\subdistname
are
just formal expressions,
i.e.

\mathcal{O}
could be alternatively (but
less clearly) defined as

(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N})\uplus\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 1
.

The order
>^{\mathcal{O}}
on the set
\mathcal{O}
is well-founded.

Proof 3.1
.

Let us consider the set
\mathbb{N}
equipped with the
standard order
>_{\mathbb{N}}
on natural numbers. Let us also consider
the set
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}:=\mathbb{N}\uplus\{\infty\}
equipped with the
order
>_{\infty}:=>_{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\langle\infty,n\rangle\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Since
>_{\mathbb{N}}
and
>_{\infty}
are both WF, then the
lexicographic order induced by
\langle>_{\mathbb{N}},>_{\infty}\rangle
on

\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
, written

>_{\texttt{LEX}}
, is also WF. We show that
>^{\mathcal{O}}
is WF
by projecting it into the WF order
>_{\texttt{LEX}}
,
i.e. we define a projection function
\subproj

such that
s>^{\mathcal{O}}s^{\prime}
implies
\subproj(s)>_{\texttt{LEX}}\subproj(s^{\prime})
, for
any
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
.
Let us define
\subproj(s)=\langle\mathbb{L}(s),\mathbb{S}(s)\rangle
, where

\mathbb{L}(\subesname(k,n))\coloneqq k
and
\mathbb{L}(\subdistname(k))\coloneqq k
while

\mathbb{S}(\subesname(k,n))\coloneqq n
and
\mathbb{S}(\subdistname(k))\coloneqq\infty
.
We reason by cases.

•

s_{0}=\subesname(k,n)>^{\mathcal{O}}\subesname(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})=s_{1}
. Then
\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{0}),\mathbb{S}(s_{0})\rangle=\langle k,n\rangle>_{\texttt{LEX}}\langle k^{\prime},n^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{1}),\mathbb{S}(s_{1})\rangle
holds by definition since
either
k>k^{\prime}
or
k=k^{\prime}
and
n>n^{\prime}
.

•

s_{0}=\subdistname(k)>^{\mathcal{O}}\subdistname(k^{\prime})=s_{1}
. Then
\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{0}),\mathbb{S}(s_{0})\rangle=\langle k,\infty\rangle>_{\texttt{LEX}}\langle k^{\prime},\infty\rangle=\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{1}),\mathbb{S}(s_{1})\rangle
holds by definition since
k>k^{\prime}
.

•

s_{0}=\subesname(k,n)>^{\mathcal{O}}\subdistname(k^{\prime})=s_{1}
. Then
\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{0}),\mathbb{S}(s_{0})\rangle=\langle k,n\rangle>_{\texttt{LEX}}\langle k^{\prime},\infty\rangle=\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{1}),\mathbb{S}(s_{1})\rangle
holds by definition since
k>k^{\prime}
.

•

s_{0}=\subdistname(k)>^{\mathcal{O}}\subesname(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})=s_{1}
. Then
\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{0}),\mathbb{S}(s_{0})\rangle=\langle k,\infty\rangle>_{\texttt{LEX}}\langle k^{\prime},n^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{L}(s_{1}),\mathbb{S}(s_{1})\rangle
holds by definition
since either
k>k^{\prime}
or
k=k^{\prime}
and
\infty>n
.

We write
>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}
for the multiset extension of the
order
>^{\mathcal{O}}
on
\mathcal{O}
, which turns out to be
well-founded
[
BN98
]
by
1
.
Some operations on multisets are needed to build the measure

\mathsf{CL}\left(\_\right)
on terms. Indeed,
let
M
be a multiset of objects in
\mathcal{O}
.
Multiset sum is denoted
\sqcup
. Furthermore:

(1)

The
\subesname
-elements
(resp.
\subdistname
-elements
) of
the multiset
M
are all the objects of the form

\subesname(k,n)
(resp.
\subdistname(k)
) in
M
. We then may write

M
as
M_{\subesname}\sqcup M_{\subdistname}
, where
M_{\subesname}
(resp.

M_{\subdistname}
)
contains all the
\subesname
-elements (resp
\subdistname
-elements) of
M
.

(2)

Given
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
M^{\leq K}
(resp.
M^{>K}
)
for the multiset containing all
o\in M
such that the first element of

o
is less than
K
(resp. strictly greater than
K
).
We write
M^{>K}_{\subesname}
for
M^{>K}\sqcap M_{\subesname}
,
where
\sqcap
denotes multiset intersection.

(3)

M
can thus be decomposed in three disjoint multisets

M_{\subdistname},M_{\subesname}^{\leq K}
and
M_{\subesname}^{>K}
, for every
K\in\mathbb{N}
.

(4)

We also define the following operation on
M
:

p\cdot M\coloneqq[\subesname(p+k,n)\mid\subesname(k,n)\in M]\sqcup[\subdistname(p+k)\mid\subdistname(k)\in M]

We are now ready to (inductively) define our
cuts level
measure

\mathsf{CL}\left(\cdot\right)
on terms.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}l@{\hspace{1cm}}l}\mathsf{CL}\left(x\right)\coloneqq[\,]\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\mathsf{CL}\left(\lambda x.t\right)\coloneqq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\mathsf{CL}\left(tu\right)\coloneqq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{CL}\left(t[x/u]\right)\coloneqq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup((\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right))\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|)]\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{CL}\left(t[x/\!\!/u]\right)\coloneqq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right))\sqcup[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

Intuitively, the integer
k
in
\subesname(k,n)

and
\subdistname(k)
counts the level of variables bound by explicit substitutions,
while
n
counts the size of terms to be substituted by an ES.
Remark that for every pure term
p
we have
\mathsf{CL}\left(p\right)=[\,]
.

{exa}

Consider the following reduction sequence:

\displaystyle t_{0}=(yy)[y/(\lambda z.x)w]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}(y_{1}y_{2})(y_{1}y_{2})[y_{1}/\lambda z.x][y_{2}/w]

\displaystyle=t_{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}(y_{1}w)(y_{1}w)[y_{1}/\lambda z.x]

\displaystyle=t_{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}(y_{1}w)(y_{1}w)[y_{1}/\!\!/\lambda z.x^{\prime}[x^{\prime}/x]]

\displaystyle=t_{3}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}((\lambda z.x^{\prime})w)((\lambda z.x^{\prime})w)[x^{\prime}/x]

\displaystyle=t_{4}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}((\lambda z.x)w)((\lambda z.x)w)

\displaystyle=t_{5}

We have
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{0}\right)=[\subesname(1,4)]
,

\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{1}\right)=[\subesname(1,1),\subesname(1,2)]
,

\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{2}\right)=[\subesname(1,2)]
,

\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{3}\right)=[\subesname(1,1),\subdistname(0)]
,

\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{4}\right)=[\subesname(1,1)]
and
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{5}\right)=[\,]
.

Fact 2
.

Some properties on multisets are straightforward:

•

If
M_{1}>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}M_{2}
, then
M_{1}\sqcup M>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}M_{2}\sqcup M
.

•

If
M_{1}>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}M_{2}
, then
k\cdot M_{1}>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}k\cdot M_{2}

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

k_{1}\cdot k_{2}\cdot M=(k_{1}+k_{2})\cdot M
.

Lemma 3
.

If
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)
and
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\geq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u)
holds for every

x\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{L})
, then
\mathsf{CL}\left(\mathtt{L}\langle t\rangle\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}\mathsf{CL}\left(\mathtt{L}\langle u\rangle\right)
.

Proof 3.2
.

By induction on
\mathtt{L}
. The property is straightforward.

{restatable}

lemmssubstitution
Let
t
be a term,
x
a variable and
p
a pure term.
Let
K=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)
.
Then there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}\sqcup[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq K\mbox{ and }n\leq N]
.

Proof 3.3
.

By induction on
t

(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:ms-substitution).

{restatable}

lemmspi

Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
.
Then
t\rightarrow_{\pi}t^{\prime}
implies
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\geq^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof 3.4
.

By induction on the reduction
t\rightarrow_{\pi}t^{\prime}
.
We only detail here the case where

t=s[y/r[x/u]]\mapsto_{\pi}s[y/r][x/u]=t^{\prime}
at root,
where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)

(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:ms-stable-by-pi).

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(r[x/u]\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1,|r[x/u]|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1)\cdot\left(\mathsf{CL}\left(r\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+1,|u|)]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1,|r[x/u]|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(r\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+2)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+2,|u|),\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1,|r[x/u]|)]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(r\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1,|r[x/u]|)]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+2)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+2,|u|)]

\displaystyle>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}\left(\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(r\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+1,|r|)]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s[y/r])+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s[y/r])+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)

The
>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}
inequation is justified by the following facts:

•

|r[x/u]|>|r|
.

•

\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+2=\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{y}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(r)+1)+1=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s[y/r])+1
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
.
Then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
implies
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof 3.5
.

Let
t=\mathtt{C}\langle t_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}\mathtt{C}\langle t_{1}\rangle=t^{\prime}
,
where
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
is a reduction step at the root
position. We proceed by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.
We detail the base case which is
\mathtt{C}=\Diamond
. In all such
cases we use
Section 3
to push
\mathtt{L}
outside, i.e. we
can write
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
as
t_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\pi}\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle=t_{1}
,
where
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}t^{\prime}_{1}
is a root step which does not
push any list context outside. We then show the property for root
steps
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}t^{\prime}_{1}
, and we conclude
with
Section 3
then
3

by
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{0}\right)\geq^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{1}\right)
since
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t^{\prime}_{0})\geq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t^{\prime}_{1})
holds for every

x\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{L})
by
Subsection 2.3
.
Let us analyse all the cases
t_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}t_{1}^{\prime}
.

(1)

If
t^{\prime}_{0}=t[x/us]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]=t^{\prime}_{1}
,
where
y
and
z
are fresh variables,
then

\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{0}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(us\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|us|)]\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/yz\}[y/u]\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+1,|s|)]

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/yz\}\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+1,|u|)])

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+1,|s|)]

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/yz\}\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|)])

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|s|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/yz\}\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(us\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|),\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|s|)]

By
Section 3
,

\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/yz\}\right)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subesname}^{>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)}\sqcup[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We also have that
[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|us|)]>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|),\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|s|)]>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqsupseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subesname}^{>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)}

and
[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|us|)]>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]
.
Thus we conclude
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{0}^{\prime}\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{1}^{\prime}\right)
.

(2)

If
t^{\prime}_{0}=t[x/\lambda y.u]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]]=t^{\prime}_{1}
,
then

\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{0}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|+1)]\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(\lambda y.z[z/u]\right)\sqcup[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(z[z/u]\right)\sqcup[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\cdot(\mathsf{CL}\left(z\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(z)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(z)+1,|u|)])\sqcup[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\cdot(1\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(1,|u|)])\sqcup[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|),\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]

\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{0}\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{1}\right)
because the multisets are
the same except for
\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|)

and
\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))
on the right
which are smaller than

\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|+1)
on the left.

(3)

If
t^{\prime}_{0}=t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}t\{x/\lambda y.u\}=t^{\prime}_{1}
, then we have
\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{0}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))].
By

Section 3
,
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{1}^{\prime}\right)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)}\sqcup[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
[\subdistname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))]>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]
and
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqsupseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)}
, then we conclude
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{0}^{\prime}\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{1}^{\prime}\right)
.

(4)

If
t^{\prime}_{0}=t[x/y]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}t\{x/y\}=t^{\prime}_{1}
,
we have
\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{0}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,1)]
.
By
Section 3
,

\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}_{1}\right)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subesname}^{>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)}\sqcup[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since

[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,1)]>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}[\subesname(k,n)\mid k\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),n\leq N]
and
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqsupseteq\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)}
, we conclude
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{0}^{\prime}\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{1}^{\prime}\right)

The sequence of
Section 3
illustrates this phenomenon:
indeed,
\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{i}\right)>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}\mathsf{CL}\left(t_{i+1}\right)
for

0\leq i<5
.

Corollary 5
.

The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
is terminating.

Simulations

We show the relation between

\lambda R
and
\lambda
,
as well as the atomic
\lambda
-calculus
\lambda a
.
For that, we introduce a projection from
\mathtt{T}_{R}
to

\mathtt{T}_{P}
implementing the unfolding of all the explicit cuts:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}x^{\downarrow}\coloneqq x&(\lambda x.t)^{\downarrow}\coloneqq\lambda x.t^{\downarrow}&(tu)^{\downarrow}\coloneqq t^{\downarrow}u^{\downarrow}&(t[x\triangleleft u])^{\downarrow}\coloneqq t^{\downarrow}\{x/u^{\downarrow}\}.\end{array}

Thus
e.g.

x[x/z[y/w]][w/w^{\prime}]^{\downarrow}=x\{x/z\{y/w\}\}\{w/w^{\prime}\}=z
.
The previous projection can be extended from list contexts to
substitutions as follows:

\Diamond^{\downarrow}:=\{\}
and

(\mathtt{L}[x\triangleleft u])^{\downarrow}:=\mathtt{L}^{\downarrow}\circ\{x/u^{\downarrow}\}
,
where
\circ
denotes standard composition of substitutions.

{restatable}

lemsimullres
Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
.
If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}t^{\prime\downarrow}
. In particular, if either
t\rightarrow_{\pi}t^{\prime}
or
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\downarrow}=t^{\prime\downarrow}
.

Proof 3.6
.

By induction on the stated relations.

The relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
enjoys

full composition
on
pure
terms.
Namely:

Lemma 6
.

For any
p\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
,
t[x/p]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t\{x/p\}
.

Proof 3.7
.

By induction on
p
.

•

If
p=y
, then

t[x/y]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}t\{x/y\}
.

•

If
p=p_{1}p_{2}
, then

\displaystyle t[x/p_{1}p_{2}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}t\{x/yz\}[y/p_{1}][z/p_{2}]

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\textit{i.h.}}^{+}t\{x/yz\}\{y/p_{1}\}[z/p_{2}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\textit{i.h.}}^{+}t\{x/yz\}\{y/p_{1}\}\{z/p_{2}\}

\displaystyle=t\{x/p_{1}p_{2}\}

•

If
p=\lambda y.q
, then

t[x/\lambda y.q]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/q]]\twoheadrightarrow_{\textit{i.h.}}^{+}t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.q]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}t\{x/\lambda y.q\}
.

This property does not hold in general if
p
is not pure.
Indeed, if
t=xx
, then
(xx)[x/y[y/z]]

does not
\mathtt{sub}
-reduce to
(y[y/z])(y[y/z])
, but to

(yy)[y/z]
. However, full composition restricted to
pure terms is sufficient to prove simulation of the
\lambda
-calculus.

Lemma 7

(Simulation of the
\lambda
-calculus)
.

Let
p_{0}\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
.
If
p_{0}\rightarrow_{\beta}p_{1}
,
then
p_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}^{+}p_{1}
.

Proof 3.8
.

Let
p_{0}=\mathtt{C}\langle t_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\beta}\mathtt{C}\langle t_{1}\rangle=p_{1}
, where

t_{0}=(\lambda x.q)p\mapsto_{\beta}q\{x/p\}=t_{1}
.
By
6
,
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}q[x/p]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t_{1}
.
The inductive cases for
\mathtt{C}
are straightforward.

The previous results have an important consequence relating the
atomic
\lambda
-calculus and the
\lambda R
-calculus. Indeed, it can
be shown that reduction in the atomic
\lambda
-calculus is
captured by
\lambda a
, and vice-versa.
More precisely, the
\lambda R
-calculus can be
simulated into the atomic
\lambda
-calculus by

Section 3
and
[
GHP13a
]
, while the
converse holds by
[
GHP13a
]
and

7
.

A more structural correspondence between
\lambda R
and
\lambda a
could also be
established. Indeed,
\lambda R
can be first refined into a
(non-linear) calculus
without
distance, let say
\lambda R^{\prime}
,
so that permutation rules are integrated in the intermediate
calculus as independent rules. Then a structural relation can be
established between
\lambda R
and
\lambda R^{\prime}
on one side, and

\lambda R^{\prime}
and the atomic
\lambda
-calculus on the other side (as
for example done in
[
KL07
]
for the
\lambda
-calculus).

Confluence

By
5
the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
is terminating.
It is then not difficult to prove confluence of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}

by using the unfolding function
\cdot^{\downarrow}
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
. Then
t
is in
\mathtt{sub}
-nf if and only if
t
is pure.

Proof 3.9
.

It is obvious that a pure term is
\mathtt{sub}
-normal.
Let us show the left-to-right implication
and consider a
\mathtt{sub}
-normal term
t
.
We reason by induction on
t
.
Suppose that
t
is not pure, so that
t=\mathtt{C}\langle t_{0}[x\triangleleft u]\rangle
.
If the explicit cut is an explicit substitution, then one of the
rules
\mathtt{app},\mathtt{dist},\mathtt{var}
apply, which contradicts the
hypothesis. Otherwise the cut is a distributor, and
u
is an
abstraction
\lambda y.u^{\prime}
, where
u^{\prime}
is in particular a

\mathtt{sub}
-normal form.
By the i.h.
u^{\prime}
is pure so that the rule
\mathtt{abs}
applies,
which contradicts the hypothesis again.

Corollary 9
.

Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
. If
t
is in
\mathtt{sub}
-nf, then

t^{\downarrow}=t
.

Lemma 10
.

The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
is terminating and confluent.

Proof 3.10
.

Termination holds by
5
.
For confluence, suppose
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
and
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{2}
.
Let
t_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}_{1}
and

t_{2}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}_{2}
, where

t^{\prime}_{1}
and
t^{\prime}_{2}
are in
\mathtt{sub}
-nf.
Then by
9
,
(t_{i}^{\prime})^{\downarrow}=t_{i}^{\prime}

for both
i=1,2
.
By
Section 3
,

(t_{i}^{\prime})^{\downarrow}=t_{i}^{\downarrow}=t^{\downarrow}
so that
t_{1}^{\prime}=t_{2}^{\prime}
, closing the diagram.

By termination of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
any
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
has
a
\mathtt{sub}
-nf, and by confluence this
\mathtt{sub}
-nf is
unique.
By
Section 3
and
9
one obtains:

Corollary 11
.

Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
. Then the unique
\mathtt{sub}
-nf of
t
is
t^{\downarrow}
.

Theorem 12
.

The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}
is confluent.

Proof 3.11
.

Let
t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
such that
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}t_{1}
and
t\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}t_{2}
. By
simulation (
Section 3
), we have
t^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}t_{1}^{\downarrow}
and
t^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}t_{2}^{\downarrow}
. By

10
, there exist
t^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
t^{\prime}_{2}
) the unique
\mathtt{sub}
-nf of
t_{1}
(resp.
t_{2}
).
By
11
we have

t^{\prime}_{1}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}
and
t^{\prime}_{2}=t_{2}^{\downarrow}
.
Because
\rightarrow_{\beta}
is confluent, there is
u
such
that
t_{1}^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}u
and
t_{2}^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}u
,
and by

7
,
t_{1}^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}u
and
t_{2}^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}u
. The diagram is then closed by
t_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}_{1}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}u
and
t_{2}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}_{2}=t_{2}^{\downarrow}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}u
. Graphically,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}_{1}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}^{\downarrow}=t^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{R}}

4.
Encoding Evaluation Strategies

In the theory of programming languages
[
Plo75
]
, the notion of

calculus
is usually based on a non-deterministic rewriting
relation, while the deterministic notion of
strategy
is
associated to a concrete machinery being able to implement a specific
evaluation procedure. Typical evaluation strategies are call-by-name,
call-by-value and call-by-need, to name a few.

Although the atomic
\lambda
-calculus was
introduced as a technical tool to implement full laziness, only
its (non-deterministic) equational theory
was studied. In this paper we bridge the gap between
the theoretical presentation of the atomic
\lambda
-calculus and concrete
specifications of evaluation strategies. Indeed, we use the

\lambda R
-calculus to investigate two concrete cases: a call-by-name strategy
implementing weak head reduction, based on full substitution, and the
call-by-need fully lazy strategy, which uses linear
substitution.

In this work, we choose to implement full laziness for pure terms, that is, for
the usual
\lambda
-calculus without cuts.
Indeed, we see explicit cuts as a tool for a fully lazy implementation of the
\lambda
-calculus.
We thus keep in line with the definitions found in the literature.
Defining full laziness for terms with explicit cuts also brings technical
difficulties, which might divert from the main point: using node replication to
implement a fully lazy strategy.

We then restrict the set of terms to a subset

U
, which simplifies the formal reasoning of explicit cuts inside
distributors. Indeed, distributors will all be of the
shape
[x/\!\!/\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle]
, where
p
is a pure
term containing the constructors that have been (symbolically) shared in the
distributor, and
\mathtt{LL}
is a
commutative list
(defined
below).
We argue that this restriction is natural
in a weak implementation of the
\lambda
-calculus: it
is true on pure terms and is preserved through
evaluation.
We consider the following grammars.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\mbox{({Linear Cut Values})}&\texttt{T}&\Coloneqq&\lambda x.\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle\mbox{ where }y\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL})\implies|p|_{y}=1\\
\mbox{({Commutative Lists})}&\mathtt{LL}&\Coloneqq&\Diamond\mid\mathtt{LL}[x/p]\mid\mathtt{LL}[x/\!\!/\texttt{T}]\mbox{ where }|\mathtt{LL}|_{x}=0\\
\mbox{({Values})}&v&\Coloneqq&\lambda x.p\\
\mbox{({Restricted Terms})}&\texttt{U}&\Coloneqq&x\mid v\mid\texttt{U}\ \texttt{U}\mid\texttt{U}[x/\texttt{U}]\mid\texttt{U}[x/\!\!/\texttt{T}]\end{array}

A term
t
generated by any of the grammars
G
defined above is
written
t\in G
.
Thus
e.g.

\lambda x.(yz)[y/\mathtt{I}][z/\mathtt{I}]\in\texttt{T}

but
\lambda x.(yy)[y/\mathtt{I}]\notin\texttt{T}
,

\Diamond[x/yz][x^{\prime}/\mathtt{I}]\in\mathtt{LL}

but
\Diamond[x/yz][y/\mathtt{I}]\notin\mathtt{LL}
,
and
(yz)[y/\!\!/\mathtt{I}]\in\texttt{U}
but

(yz)[y/\!\!/\lambda x.(yy)[y/\mathtt{I}]]\notin\texttt{U}
.

The set
T
is stable by the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
(
13
),
but
U
is clearly not stable under the whole
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}
relation, where
\mathtt{dB}
-reductions
may occur under abstractions. For instance, let

t_{1}=(yz)[y/\!\!/\lambda x.(\lambda y.yy)\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}(yz)[y/\!\!/\lambda x.(yy)[y/\mathtt{I}]]=t_{2}
.
Then
t_{1}\in\texttt{U}
but
t_{2}\notin\texttt{U}
, since
|yy|_{y}=2
.
However,
U
is stable under both weak strategies
to be defined: call-by-name and call-by-need.
We factorize the proofs by proving stability for a more general relation

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{F}}
, defined
as the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}
with
\mathtt{dB}
-reductions forbidden under abstractions and inside
distributors.

Lemma 13
.

If
t\in\texttt{T}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{T}
.

Proof 4.1
.

We first show a more general statement, namely that

t=\mathtt{LL}_{0}\langle p_{0}\rangle
with
|p_{0}|_{y}=1

for every
y\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}_{0})
,
and
t\mapsto_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
imply

t^{\prime}=\mathtt{LL}_{1}\langle p_{1}\rangle

with
|p_{1}|_{y}=1
for every
y\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}_{1})
.
In the following rules
\mathtt{var}
,
\mathtt{app}
and
\mathtt{dist}
, there is no
\mathtt{L}
context inside the explicit substitutions because lists in
\mathtt{LL}

only contain pure terms by definition.

•

t=u[x/z]\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}}u\{x/z\}=t^{\prime}
.
This is straightforward.

•

t=\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle[x/q_{1}q_{2}]\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}}\mathtt{LL}\langle p\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}\rangle[x_{1}/q_{1}][x_{2}/q_{2}]=t^{\prime}
.
Freshness of both
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
implies
|p\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}|_{x_{1}}=|p\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}|_{x_{2}}=|p|_{x}=1
,
and
|q_{1}|_{x_{2}}=0
.

•

t=\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle[x/\lambda z.p^{\prime}]\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}}\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda z.w[w/p^{\prime}]]=t^{\prime}
.
By hypothesis
|p|_{x}=1
,

|\mathtt{LL}|_{x}=0
and
\lambda z.p^{\prime}
is pure. Then,

\lambda z.w[w/p^{\prime}]\in\texttt{T}
because

p^{\prime}
is pure and
|w|_{w}=1
.

•

t=\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda z.\mathtt{LL}^{\prime}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle]\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}\mathtt{LL}^{\prime}\langle\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle\{x/\lambda z.p^{\prime}\}\rangle=t^{\prime}
.
By hypothesis
\lambda z.\mathtt{LL}^{\prime}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle\in\texttt{T}
thus
\lambda z.p^{\prime}

and
p\{x/\lambda z.p^{\prime}\}
are pure. We conclude since
|\mathtt{LL}|_{x}=0
by hypothesis.

Now we can lift the property to
T

by observing that we necessarily have
t=\lambda x.u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}\lambda x.u^{\prime}
,
where
u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}u^{\prime}
.
Then we conclude by the previous point.

Lemma 14
.

If
t\in\texttt{U}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{F}}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{U}
.

Proof 4.2
.

Straightforward by induction on the reduction relation.

4.1.
Call-by-name

The
call-by-name
(CBN) strategy
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}

(
Figure 1
) is defined on the set of terms
U
as
the union of the following relations
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}
.
The strategy is
weak
as there is no reduction under abstractions.
It is also worth noticing (as a particular case of
14
)
that
t\in\texttt{U}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}t^{\prime}
implies
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{U}
.

\frac{t\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}}{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=({db})}\frac{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}}{tu\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}u}\quad\textsc{right=({appdb})}\frac{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}}{t[x\triangleleft u]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]}\quad\textsc{right=({subdb})}\\
\frac{t\mapsto_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}}{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=({s})}\frac{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}}{tu\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}u}\quad\textsc{right=({apps})}\frac{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}}{u[x/\!\!/\lambda y.t]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}u[x/\!\!/\lambda y.t^{\prime}]}\quad\textsc{right=({subs})}

Figure 1.
Call-by-Name Strategy

{exa}

This example follows a call-by-name evaluation.
The name of the contextual rule is written in the superscript of the
arrow symbol, and the redex is underlined.

\begin{array}[]{rll}\underline{(\lambda x_{1}.\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))(\lambda y.(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})y)}&\rightarrow^{\textsc{db}}&\underline{(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\lambda y.(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})y]}\\
&\rightarrow^{\textsc{s}}&(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.\underline{z[z/(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})y]}]\\
&\rightarrow^{\textsc{subs}}&(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.\underline{(z_{1}z_{2})[z_{1}/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][z_{2}/y]}]\\
&\rightarrow^{\textsc{subs}}&\underline{(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.(z_{1}y)[z_{1}/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]]}\\
&\rightarrow^{\textsc{s}}&(\underline{\mathtt{I}((\lambda y.z_{1}y)\mathtt{I})})[z_{1}/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow^{\textsc{subdb}}&x_{2}[x_{2}/(\lambda y.z_{1}y)\mathtt{I}][z_{1}/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\\
&\twoheadrightarrow^{+}&((\lambda y.z_{1}y)\mathtt{I})[z_{1}/\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\\
&\twoheadrightarrow^{+}&\lambda y.(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})y\end{array}

The strategy
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
does not impose duplication of all nodes in the
body of an abstraction inside the distributor: only the skeleton of the
abstraction
\lambda y.(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})y
is replicated.
But the strategy forbids
\mathtt{dB}
-reductions inside explicit cuts, so that there is
no benefit gained by keeping shared terms such as
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
.
Indeed, the main idea behind full laziness is that shared terms are only reduced once.
The CBN strategy, on the contrary, duplicates arguments before reducing them.
The absence of optimization is reflected by the fact that the strategy, although not deterministic,
enjoys the remarkable
diamond
property, guaranteeing in particular that
all reduction sequences starting from
t
and ending in a normal form have the
same length.

{restatable}

[Diamond]propdiamond

The CBN strategy enjoys the diamond property,

i.e.
for any terms
t,u,s\in\texttt{U}

such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}u
,
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}s
and
u\neq s
,
there exists
t^{\prime}
such that
u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}t^{\prime}
and
s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}t^{\prime}
.

Proof 4.3
.

By separate inductions on the reduction relations

\langle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}\rangle
,
\langle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}\rangle
and
\langle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}},\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}\rangle
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:diamond).

It is worth noticing that call-by-name in the
\lambda
-calculus
can be simulated by call-by-name in
\lambda R
.
The former can be defined by weak-head reduction, denoted
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}
, and generated by the following rules:

\frac{\phantom{}}{(\lambda y.t)u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t\{x/u\}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t^{\prime}}{tu\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t^{\prime}u}

There is in particular a one-to-one relation between
\beta
-steps and

\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}
-steps.

Lemma 15

(Relating Call-by-Name Strategies)
.

•

Let
p_{0}\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
. If
p_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}p_{1}
, then

p_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}p_{1}
(thus
p_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}^{+}p_{1}
).

•

Let
t_{0}\in\texttt{U}
. If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t_{1}
, then

t_{0}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t_{1}^{\downarrow}
.
If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
, then
t_{0}^{\downarrow}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}
.

Proof 4.4
.

•

By induction on
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}
.

–

Let
p_{0}=(\lambda x.p)q\rightarrow_{\beta}p\{x/q\}=p_{1}
. Then
(\lambda x.p)q\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}p[x/q]
and we need to verify that
p[x/q]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}p\{x/q\}
. The proof of
t[x/q]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t\{x/q\}
for any
t\in\texttt{U}
and pure term
q
is by induction on
q
:

*

If
q=y
then
t[x/y]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t\{x/y\}
.

*

If
q=q_{0}q_{1}
then
t[x/q]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t\{x/z_{0}z_{1}\}[z_{0}/q_{0}][z_{1}/q_{1}]
. By the i.h. we have

\begin{array}[]{c}t\{x/z_{0}z_{1}\}[z_{0}/q_{0}][z_{1}/q_{1}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}(t\{x/z_{0}z_{1}\}[z_{0}/q_{0}])\{z_{1}/q_{1}\}=t\{x/z_{0}q_{1}\}[z_{0}/q_{0}]\\
\mbox{ and }\\
t\{x/z_{0}q_{1}\}[z_{0}/q_{0}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t\{x/z_{0}q_{1}\}\{z_{0}/q_{0}\}=t\{x/q_{0}q_{1}\}\end{array}

Therefore,
t[x/q_{0}q_{1}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t\{x/q_{0}q_{1}\}
.

*

If
q=\lambda y.q^{\prime}
then
t[x/q]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/q^{\prime}]]
.
By the i.h. we have that
z[z/q^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}z\{z/q^{\prime}\}=q^{\prime}
thus
t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/q^{\prime}]]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.q^{\prime}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t\{x/\lambda y.q^{\prime}\}
. Therefore,
t[x/\lambda y.q^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}^{+}t\{x/\lambda y.q^{\prime}\}
.

–

Let
p_{0}=pq\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}p^{\prime}q=p_{1}
where
p\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}p^{\prime}
. By the
i.h. we have that
p\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}^{+}p^{\prime}
then, by
(\textsc{appdb})
and

(\textsc{apps})
,
p_{0}=pq\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}^{+}p^{\prime}q=p_{1}
.

•

By case analysis on
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
. If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
then
t_{0}^{\downarrow}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}
by
Section 3
. If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t_{1}
then we prove the property by induction on
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}
.

–

Let
t_{0}=(\lambda x.t)u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t[x/u]=t_{1}
. Then

t_{0}^{\downarrow}=(\lambda x.t^{\downarrow})u^{\downarrow}\rightarrow_{\beta}t^{\downarrow}\{x/u^{\downarrow}\}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}
. Note that both
t^{\downarrow}
and
u^{\downarrow}
are pure terms.

–

Let
t_{0}=tu\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}u=t_{1}
where
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}
. Then

t^{\downarrow}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t^{\prime\downarrow}
by the i.h., thus
t_{0}^{\downarrow}=t^{\downarrow}u^{\downarrow}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t^{\prime\downarrow}u^{\downarrow}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}
.

–

Let
t_{0}=t[x\triangleleft u]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]=t_{1}
where
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}
.
Then
t^{\downarrow}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t^{\prime\downarrow}
by the i.h., thus
t_{0}^{\downarrow}=t^{\downarrow}\{x/u^{\downarrow}\}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}t^{\prime\downarrow}\{x/u^{\downarrow}\}=t_{1}^{\downarrow}
. Note that the result depends on the closure of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{whr}}
by (implicit) substitutions, which has a straightforward proof by induction on (pure) term
t^{\downarrow}
, using substitution composition.

The following grammar
Na
is meant to characterize
normal forms with respect to the
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
strategy:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\texttt{Na}&\Coloneqq&\lambda x.p\mid\overline{\texttt{Na}}\\
\overline{\texttt{Na}}&\Coloneqq&x\mid\overline{\texttt{Na}}\,t\end{array}

Notice that all normal forms are pure terms: we unfold all explicit
substitutions with
\mathtt{sub}
-steps.

Lemma 16
.

Let
t\in\texttt{U}
. Then
t\in\texttt{Na}
iff
t
is in
\mathtt{name}
-nf.

Proof 4.5
.

The left-to-right implication is straightforward.
The right-to-left implication proof is by induction on
U
.

•

t=x
. By definition,
t\in\overline{\texttt{Na}}
.

•

t=\lambda x.p
. Then
t\in\texttt{Na}
by definition.

•

t=t^{\prime}u
, where
t^{\prime},u\in\texttt{U}
.
By definition of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
,
t
in

\mathtt{name}
-nf implies
t^{\prime}
is also in
\mathtt{name}
-nf
and
t^{\prime}
is neither an
explicit cut nor an abstraction. Thus
t^{\prime}\in\overline{\texttt{Na}}

by the i.h. and we can conclude
t\in\overline{\texttt{Na}}
.

•

t=t^{\prime}[x/u]
, where
t^{\prime},u\in\texttt{U}
.
This is not possible because there is always an
applicable structural rule which
would contradict
t
to be in
\mathtt{name}
-nf.

•

t=t^{\prime}[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]
, where
\lambda y.u=\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle\in\texttt{T}
.
Then either we can apply a structural rule
on
u
,
or
u
is pure (i.e.
\mathtt{LL}=\Diamond
) and we can apply rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}
. In both cases we would have a contradiction with

t
in
\mathtt{name}
-nf.

4.2.
