Call-by-need

We now specify a deterministic strategy
\mathtt{flneed}
implementing
demand-driven computations and only linearly replicating nodes of
values

(
i.e.
pure
abstractions). Given a value
\lambda x.p
, only the piece of structure
containing the paths between the binder
\lambda x
and all the free
occurrences of
x
in
p
, named
skeleton
, will be copied. All
the other components of the abstraction will remain shared, thus
avoiding some future duplications of redexes, as explained in the
introduction.
By copying only the smallest possible substructure of the abstraction,
the strategy
\mathtt{flneed}
implements an optimization of call-by-need
called
f
ully
l
azy sharing

[
Wad71
]
.
First, we formally define the key notions we are going to use.

A
free expression

[
Jon87
,
Bal12a
]

of a
pure
term
p
is a strict subterm
q
of
p
such that
every free occurrence of a variable in
q
is also
a free occurrence of the variable in
p
. A

free expression
of
p
is
maximal
if it is not a subterm
of another free expression of
p
. From now on, we will consider the
(ordered) list of all maximal free expressions (MFE) of a term.
Thus
e.g.
the MFEs of
\lambda y.p
, where
p=(\mathtt{I}y)\mathtt{I}(\lambda z.zyw)
,
is given by the list

[\mathtt{I};\mathtt{I};w]
.

An
n
-ary (pure) context
(
n\geq 0
) is a
(pure) context with
n
holes
\Diamond
. A skeleton is
an
n
-ary pure context where the maximal free expressions w.r.t. a
variable set
\theta
are replaced with holes. We introduce two
different yet equivalent notions of skeleton, together with a
corresponding operation of skeleton extraction: we argue that they
entail respectively a big-step and a small-step semantics. More
precisely, in the big-step semantics the skeleton extraction process
can be seen as a meta-operator, defined by operations that are
external to the calculus itself, as in
[
AF97
]
, while in the
small-step semantics the process of extraction is defined by an
explicit reduction relation encoded in the calculus itself.

A first definition of skeleton

The first notion of skeleton runs as follows. Given any set of variables
\theta
,
the
\theta
-skeleton
of a pure term
is an n-ary pure (
i.e.
without explicit cuts) context defined as

\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}\coloneqq\Diamond
if
\theta\cap\operatorname{fv}(p)=\emptyset
; otherwise:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{3em}}c@{\hspace{3em}}c}\{\!\{{x}\}\!\}^{\theta}\coloneqq x\hfil\hskip 30.00005pt&\{\!\{{\lambda x.p}\}\!\}^{\theta}\coloneqq\lambda x.\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta\cup\{x\}}\hfil\hskip 30.00005pt&\{\!\{{p_{1}p_{2}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\coloneqq\{\!\{{p_{1}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\{\!\{{p_{2}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\end{array}

Thus
e.g.
if
p=(Iy)I(\lambda z.zyw)
as above, then
\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\{y\}}=(\Diamond y)\Diamond(\lambda z.zy\Diamond)
.

Function
\{\!\{{\_}\}\!\}^{\theta}
is (implicitly) intended to give a
context whose holes correspond to the MFE’s
that are abstracted out. Splitting a term into a
skeleton and a multiset of MFEs is at the core of full laziness. This
can naturally be implemented in the node replication model, as
observed in
[
GHP13a
]
. Here, we give two different
(alternative) operational semantics to achieve it. The first one
(
Figure 2
), written
\Downarrow^{\theta}
, uses big-step semantics
and implements the first definition of skeleton introduced above.

\frac{x\text{ fresh}}{p\Downarrow^{\theta}x[x/p]}\quad\text{when $\operatorname{fv}(p)\cap\theta=\emptyset$;
otherwise:}\\
\frac{}{x\Downarrow^{\theta}x}\frac{p\Downarrow^{\theta\cup\{x\}}\mathtt{L}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle}{\lambda x.p\Downarrow^{\theta}\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda x.p^{\prime}\rangle}\frac{p\Downarrow^{\theta}\mathtt{L}_{1}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle q\Downarrow^{\theta}\mathtt{L}_{2}\langle q^{\prime}\rangle}{pq\Downarrow^{\theta}\mathtt{L}_{2}\langle\mathtt{L}_{1}\langle p^{\prime}q^{\prime}\rangle\rangle}

Figure 2.
Relation
\Downarrow^{\theta}
: Splitting Skeleton and MFEs in Big-Step Semantics

Each of the rules in
Figure 2
corresponds to a
different case in the first definition of
\theta
-skeleton. In the
first rule, since there is no free variable of
p
in
\theta
,
p
is
thus an MFE kept shared in an explicit substitution. The other
three rules correspond to each possible constructor,
where all the explicit cuts
created during the inductive cases are pushed out.

{exa}

Let
y,z\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
, so that
t
is the MFE of
\lambda y.x[x/\lambda z.(yt)z]
. Then,

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{y\Downarrow^{\{y,z\}}y}\frac{}{t\Downarrow^{\{y,z\}}x[x/t]}}{yt\Downarrow^{\{y,z\}}(yx)[x/t]}\frac{}{z\Downarrow^{\{y,z\}}z}}{(yt)z\Downarrow^{\{y,z\}}((yx)z)[x/t]}}{\lambda z.(yt)z\Downarrow^{\{y\}}(\lambda z.(yx)z)[x/t]}

Lemma 17

(Correctness of
\Downarrow^{\theta}
)
.

If
p\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
,
then
\exists n\geq 0
s.t.
p\Downarrow^{\theta}\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq n}
,
where
\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\rangle=p
,
and
(x_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
are fresh pairwise distinct variables.
Moreover,
\operatorname{fv}(t_{i})\cap\theta=\emptyset
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof 4.6
.

If
\operatorname{fv}(p)\cap\theta=\emptyset
, then
p\Downarrow^{\theta}x_{1}[x_{1}/p]

and
\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}=\Diamond
, so that
\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle p\rangle=p

trivially holds.
Otherwise, we reason by induction on
p
:

•

If
p=x
, then
\{\!\{{x}\}\!\}^{\theta}=x
,
so the property holds for
n=0
because
x\Downarrow^{\theta}x
.

•

If
p=p_{1}p_{2}
, then

\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}=\{\!\{{p_{1}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\{\!\{{p_{2}}\}\!\}^{\theta}
.
By the i.h. we have

\displaystyle p_{1}\Downarrow^{\theta}\{\!\{{p_{1}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\rangle[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq k}

\displaystyle\text{ and }p_{2}\Downarrow^{\theta}\{\!\{{p_{2}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{k<i\leq n},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\{\!\{{p_{1}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\rangle=p_{1}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\{\!\{{p_{2}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle t_{k+1},\dots,t_{n}\rangle=p_{2}.

Hence:

\displaystyle p_{1}p_{2}

\displaystyle\Downarrow^{\theta}(\{\!\{{p_{1}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\rangle\{\!\{{p_{2}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle)[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq k}[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{k<i\leq n}

\displaystyle=\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq n}

•

If
p=\lambda x.p^{\prime}
,then

\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}=\lambda x.\{\!\{{p^{\prime}}\}\!\}^{\theta\cup\{x\}}
.
By the i.h. we have

p^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\theta\cup\{x\}}\{\!\{{p^{\prime}}\}\!\}^{\theta\cup\{x\}}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq n}.

Moreover,
x\notin\bigcup_{i\leq n}\operatorname{fv}(t_{i})
by definition of
\Downarrow
and every
x_{i}
is different from
x
.
Hence:

\lambda x.p^{\prime}\Downarrow^{\theta}(\lambda x.\{\!\{{p^{\prime}}\}\!\}^{\theta\cup\{x\}}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle)[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq n}=\{\!\{{\lambda x.p^{\prime}}\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/t_{i}]_{i\leq n}.

The correcteness lemma states in particular that

p\Downarrow^{\theta}\mathtt{L}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle
implies
p^{\prime}
is pure and
\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{L})\cap\theta=\emptyset
.

An alternative definition of skeleton

An
alternative
definition of
\theta
-skeleton can be given by

removing
the maximal free expressions from a term.
Indeed, the
\theta
-skeleton

\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\theta}
of a pure term

p
, where
\theta=\{x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\}
, is the n-ary pure context

\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\theta}
such that
\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\theta}\langle q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\rangle=p
, for
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]

the maximal free expressions of
\lambda x_{1}.\ldots\lambda x_{n}.p

1
1

1

The order of the abstractions is irrelevant.
.
It is easy to show that both notions of skeleton are equivalent,
i.e.

\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}^{\theta}=\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\theta}
.
Thus, for the same
p
as before,
\lambda y.\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\{y\}}=\lambda y.(\Diamond y)\Diamond(\lambda z.zy\Diamond)
.

The second strategy to split a term into a skeleton and its MFEs is the small-step strategy

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
on the set of terms
T

(
Figure 3
), which is indeed a subset of
the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{R}}
.
It implements the second definition of skeleton we have introduced.
The relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
makes use of four basic rules which are
parameterized by the variable

y
upon which the skeleton is built, written
\mapsto^{y}
.
There are also two contextual (inductive) rules.

\frac{}{t[x/y]\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}}^{y}t\{x/y\}}\frac{y\in\operatorname{fv}(p_{1}p_{2})}{t[x/p_{1}p_{2}]\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}}^{y}t\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}[x_{1}/p_{1}][x_{2}/p_{2}]}\frac{y\in\operatorname{fv}(\lambda z.p)}{t[x/\lambda z.p]\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}}^{y}t[x/\!\!/\lambda z.w[w/p]]}\frac{y\in\operatorname{fv}(\lambda z.\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle)z\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{LL})}{t[x/\!\!/\lambda z.\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle]\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{x/\lambda z.p\}\rangle}\frac{t\mapsto^{y}t^{\prime}y\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{LL})}{\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t^{\prime}\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=({ctx1})}\frac{t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}t^{\prime}y\in\operatorname{fv}(t)y\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{LL})}{\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle u[x/\!\!/t]\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle u[x/\!\!/t^{\prime}]\rangle}\quad\textsc{right=({ctx2})}

Figure 3.
Relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
: Splitting Skeleton and MFEs in Small-Step Semantics

{exa}

Let
\lambda y.x[x/\lambda z.(yt)z]
be as in
Subsection 4.2
.

\displaystyle\lambda y.\underline{x[x/\lambda z.(yt)z]}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}^{y}\lambda y.x[x/\!\!/\lambda z.\underline{w[w/(yt)z]}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}^{z}\lambda y.x[x/\!\!/\lambda z.\underline{(w_{1}w_{2})[w_{1}/yt][w_{2}/z]}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}^{z}\lambda y.\underline{x[x/\!\!/\lambda z.{(w_{1}z)\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[w_{1}/yt]$}}]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}\lambda y.\underline{(\lambda z.{w_{1}z})[w_{1}/yt]}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}^{y}\lambda y.\underline{(\lambda z.{(x_{1}x_{2})z})[x_{1}/y]}[x_{2}/t]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}^{y}\lambda y.(\lambda z.{(yx_{2})z})[x_{2}/t]

Notice that the focused variable changes from
y
to
z
, then back to
y
.
This is because
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
constructs the innermost skeletons first.
The small-step approach allows to parametrize the reduction
relation by only one variable at a time, instead of a set.

Lemma 18
.

If
t\in\texttt{T}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{T}
.

Proof 4.7
.

For the root
\mapsto^{y}
rules, we first show that
if
t=\mathtt{LL}_{0}\langle p_{0}\rangle
with
|p_{0}|_{z}=1
for all
z\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}_{0})
, and
t\mapsto^{y}t^{\prime}
,
then
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{LL}_{1}\langle p_{1}\rangle

with
|p_{1}|_{z}=1
for all
z\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}_{1})
.

•

If
t\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}}^{y}t^{\prime}
, this is straightforward.

•

If
t=\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle[x/q_{1}q_{2}]\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}}^{y}\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}[x_{1}/q_{1}][x_{2}/q_{2}]=t^{\prime}
,
then, since
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{LL})
, we have
\mathtt{LL}\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}=\mathtt{LL}
.
Moreover, freshness of
x_{1},x_{2}

implies
|\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle|_{x_{1}}=|\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle|_{x_{2}}=|\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle|_{x}=1
, where
p^{\prime}=p\{x/x_{1}x_{2}\}
,
and
|q_{1}|_{x_{2}}=0
.

•

If
t=u[x/\lambda x^{\prime}.p]\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}}^{y}u[x/\!\!/\lambda x^{\prime}.w[w/p]]=t^{\prime}
, this is true
by hypothesis,
where in particular
|u|_{x}=1
, and

\lambda x^{\prime}.w[w/p]\in\texttt{T}
because
p

is pure and
|w|_{w}=1
.

•

If
t=\mathtt{LL}_{1}\langle p_{1}\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda z.\mathtt{LL}_{2}\langle p_{2}\rangle]\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}\mathtt{LL}_{2}\langle\mathtt{LL}_{1}\langle p_{1}\rangle\{x/\lambda z.p_{2}\}\rangle=t^{\prime}
.
By hypothesis
|p_{1}|_{x}=1
and
|\mathtt{LL}_{1}|_{x}=0
,
so that
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{LL}_{2}\langle\mathtt{LL}_{1}\langle p_{1}\{x/\lambda z.p_{2}\}\rangle\rangle=\mathtt{LL}^{\prime}_{1}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle
,
since for all
z_{1}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}_{1})
and all
z_{2}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}_{2})
,
|p_{1}|_{z_{1}}=|p_{2}|_{z_{2}}=1
and, by
\alpha
-conversion,
|p_{1}|_{z_{2}}=|p_{2}|_{z_{1}}=0

so that
|p_{1}\{x/\lambda z.p_{2}\}|_{z^{\prime}}=1

for any
z^{\prime}\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathtt{LL}^{\prime})
.

Then, for the contextual rules, we show by induction on
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
:
if
t\in\texttt{T}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{T}
.

•

In the case of
(\textsc{ctx1})
,
we have
t=\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t_{1}\rangle
.
By the hypothesis that
t\in\texttt{T}
follows
t_{0}=\mathtt{LL}_{0}\langle p_{0}\rangle
.
By the previous case analysis,
t_{1}=\mathtt{LL}_{1}\langle p_{1}\rangle
.
Therefore
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{T}
.

•

In the case of
(\textsc{ctx2})
,
we have
t=\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle u[x/\!\!/t_{0}]\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle u[x/\!\!/t_{1}]\rangle
.
By the hypothesis that
t\in\texttt{T}
follows
t_{0}\in\texttt{T}
.
By induction hypothesis,
t_{1}\in\texttt{T}
.
Therefore
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{T}
.

Lemma 19
.

The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
is confluent and terminating.

Proof 4.8
.

To show termination it is sufficient to notice that

t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}t^{\prime}
implies
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}
.
Since
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
is terminating
(
5
) then we conclude termination of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
.
Next, we show that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
is confluent
by observing that it is deterministic. Indeed,

•

The base rules
\mapsto^{y}
only reduce the outermost cut and they are all distinct:
there is one rule for an outermost distributor,
and three rules for outermost explicit substitutions,
one for each possible form (variable, application, abstraction).

•

Because of the condition
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{LL})
in rules

(\textsc{ctx1})

and
(\textsc{ctx2})

the base rules are always applied from right to left
inside an abstraction.

•

Moreover, rule
(\textsc{ctx2})
does not overlap with
any other rule, in particular with
\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}
.
Indeed, for a term
u[x/\!\!/\lambda z.\mathtt{LL}\langle p\rangle]
, there are only two possibilites.
Either
z
is a free variable of
\mathtt{LL}
, and we
cannot apply
\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}
, or
z
is not a free variable of
\mathtt{LL}
, and we can apply
\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}
.
In the latter, there is in particular no cut of
\mathtt{LL}

for which
z
is free.
Therefore, we cannot apply any base-rule recursively inside the distributor.
So, we cannot apply rule
(\textsc{ctx2})
.

Since rule application is deterministic, then there is no possible diverging diagram, and thus confluence is trivial.

Thus, from now on, we denote by
\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}

the function relating a term of
T
to its unique
\mathtt{st}
-nf.
For instance, from
Subsection 4.2
we deduce

\lambda y.x[x/\lambda z.(yt)z]\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.(\lambda z.{(yx_{2})z})[x_{2}/t]
.

Lemma 20
.

If
p
is a pure term and
\mathtt{LL}
a (commutative) list context
where
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{LL})
, then there exists
n
and an n-ary pure
context
c
such that

\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t[z/p]\rangle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/c\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle\}[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}\rangle

where the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are fresh pairwise distinct and
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]
are the MFE of
\lambda y.p
such that
c\langle q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\rangle=p
.

Proof 4.9
.

If
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(p)
, then
p
is the MFE
of
\lambda y.p
and the property is satisfied by the empty reduction,
with
n=1
,
c=\Diamond
, and
q_{1}=p
.
Otherwise, we reason by induction on
p
.

•

If
p=y
, then
\lambda y.p
has no MFE and

\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t[z/y]\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}^{y}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/y\}\rangle
.
Then the property holds for
n=0
and the 0-ary context
y
.

•

If
p=p_{1}p_{2}
, then by the i.h. on
p_{2}
and on
p_{1}
we have:

\displaystyle\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t[z/p_{1}p_{2}]\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}^{y}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/z_{1}z_{2}\}[z_{1}/p_{1}][z_{2}/p_{2}]\rangle

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/z_{1}c_{2}\langle x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle\}[z_{1}/p_{1}][x_{i}/q_{i}]_{k<i\leq n}\rangle

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/c_{1}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\rangle c_{2}\langle x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle\}[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq k}[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{k<i\leq n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/c\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle\}[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}\rangle

where
c\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle=c_{1}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\rangle c_{2}\langle x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle
, and the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are chosen to be pairwise distinct. To apply the
i.h. on
p_{1}
, we take
\mathtt{LL}
to be
\mathtt{LL}\langle\Diamond[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{k<i\leq n}\rangle
, which verifies the
hypothesis of the statement since by definition of the
MFEs,
y\notin\cup_{k<i\leq n}\operatorname{fv}(q_{i})
. We can conclude since
the maximal free
expressions of
\lambda y.p_{1}p_{2}
can be computed by
considering the MFEs of
\lambda y.p_{1}
and
\lambda y.p_{2}
respectively,
i.e.
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]
.

•

If
p=\lambda x.p^{\prime}
, then by the i.h. on
p^{\prime}
we have:

\lambda x.z^{\prime}[z^{\prime}/p^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda x.c^{\prime}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
where the terms
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]
are the MFEs of
\lambda x.p^{\prime}
, so in particular
x\notin\cup_{1\leq i\leq n}\operatorname{fv}(q_{i})
.
We can then apply
the i.h. on
q_{n},\ldots,q_{1}
, thus for
t_{0}=\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t[z/\lambda x.p^{\prime}]\rangle
we have:

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}^{y}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t[z/\!\!/\lambda x.z^{\prime}[z^{\prime}/p^{\prime}]]\rangle

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t[z/\!\!/\lambda x.c^{\prime}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}]\rangle

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}^{y}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/\lambda x.c^{\prime}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle\}[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}\rangle

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/\lambda x.c^{\prime}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},c_{n}\langle x_{n}^{1},\dots,x_{n}^{m_{n}}\rangle\rangle\}[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i<n}[x_{n}^{j}/q_{n}^{j}]_{1\leq j\leq m_{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/\lambda x.c^{\prime}\langle c_{1}\langle x_{1}^{1},\dots,x_{1}^{m_{1}}\rangle,\dots,c_{n}\langle x_{n}^{1},\dots,x_{n}^{m_{n}}\rangle\rangle\}[x_{i}^{j}/q_{n}^{j}]_{1\leq j\leq m_{i},1\leq i\leq n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle t\{z/c\langle x_{1}^{1},\dots,x_{n}^{m_{n}}\rangle\}[x_{i}^{j}/q_{n}^{j}]_{1\leq j\leq m_{i},1\leq i\leq n}\rangle

where
c\langle x_{1}^{1},\dots,x_{n}^{m_{n}}\rangle=\lambda x.c^{\prime}\langle c_{1}\langle x_{1}^{1},\dots,x_{1}^{m_{1}}\rangle,\dots,c_{n}\langle x_{n}^{1},\dots,x_{n}^{m_{n}}\rangle\rangle

and the variables
x_{1}^{1}
to
x_{n}^{m_{n}}
are taken pairwise distinct.
To apply the i.h. on
q_{k}
(
1\leq k\leq n
),
we take the linear context to be
\mathtt{LL}\langle\Diamond[x_{i}^{j}/q_{i}^{j}]_{1\leq j\leq m_{i},k<i\leq n}\rangle
, which verifies the hypothesis of the statement since by definition of
the MFEs,

y\notin\cup_{1\leq j\leq m_{i},k<i\leq n}\operatorname{fv}(q_{i}^{j})
.
By the i.h.
[q_{i}^{1};\dots;q_{i}^{m_{i}}]
are the MFEs of
\lambda y.q_{i}
for each
i
.
Therefore, since
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]
are the MFEs
of
\lambda x.p^{\prime}
, the terms
[q_{1}^{1};\dots;q_{n}^{m_{n}}]
are also the MFEs of
\lambda y.\lambda x.p^{\prime}
.

Corollary 21

(Correctness of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
)
.

Let
p\in\mathtt{T}_{P}
and
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]
be the MFEs of
\lambda y.p
.
Then
\lambda y.z[z/p]\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\{y\}}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{i\leq n}

where the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are fresh and pairwise distinct.

Proof 4.10
.

By
20
, there is an n-ary pure context
c
such that
\lambda y.z[z/p]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}t=\lambda y.c\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle[x_{i}/q_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
where
[q_{1};\dots;q_{n}]
are the MFEs of
\lambda y.p
.
Thus, by the alternative definition of skeleton,
c
is
\{\!\{\!\{{p}\}\!\}\!\}^{\{y\}}
.
Moreover,
t
is the
\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}
-nf
of
\lambda y.z[z/p]
because no more base

\mapsto^{y}
-reduction steps can be applied
to the list of explicit substitutions since
y
is not free
in
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
by definition of MFE.

From the fact that the two definitions of skeleton are
equivalent, and from both proofs of correctness
(
17
and
21
), we
infer the equivalence between the small-step and the big-step splitting
semantics (
Figure 3
and
Figure 2
resp.).
Since the small-step semantics is contained in
\lambda R
,
we use it to build our call-by-need strategy using node replication.

Another interesting question concerns the splitting semantics for terms with explicit cuts.
It is not always clear what the maximal free expressions are,
as this notion depends on the position of the explicit cuts in the
term.
For instance, take the term

t=\lambda y.z_{1}[w/xy]z_{2}
.
What should be the MFEs of
t
? It could be
[z_{1};x;z_{2}]
, or

[z_{1}z_{2};x]
, or even
[(z_{1}z_{2})[w/x]]
.
Similarly for the skeleton, should it be respectively (1)
\lambda y.\Diamond[w/x\Diamond]\Diamond
,
(2)
\lambda y.\Diamond\Diamond
or (3)
\lambda y.\Diamond
?
Solution (1) proposes to keep explicit substitutions in the skeleton.
This is not coherent with the semantics of
\lambda R
and
\lambda a
,
which only substitute pure terms.
Solution (2) consists in unfolding the explicit cuts, so that the
skeleton is pure.
This can easily be obtained by adding the following rule to the definition.

\{\!\{{t[x/u]}\}\!\}^{\theta}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{\!\{{t}\}\!\}^{\theta\cup\{x\}}\{x/\{\!\{{u}\}\!\}^{\theta}\},&\text{ if }\theta\cup\operatorname{fv}(u)\neq\emptyset\\
\{\!\{{t}\}\!\}^{\theta},&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Indeed, this is the definition of skeleton adopted for the atomic
\lambda
-calculus
in
[
GHP13a
]
,
where the authors prove that the skeleton of a term with explicit substitutions
(but without explicit distributors) can be split from the MFEs.
Unfortunately, they do not provide a splitting rewriting relation.

In cases involving explicit cuts binding no variable,
like
[w/xy]
in the term
t
above,
this definition is a cause of inefficiency: we would prefer solution (3),
which avoids duplication of the application node.
More generally, many nodes can be duplicated inside a term to reach a bound
variable that will finally be erased.
For instance, in the term
\lambda y.x_{1}[w/y]x_{2}x_{3}\dots x_{n}
,

n-1
applications nodes will need to be duplicated, and the skeleton would be
considered
\lambda y.\Diamond\Diamond\Diamond\dots\Diamond
(
n
times) following (2), and simply

\lambda y.\Diamond
following (3).
As another example, the skeleton of
\lambda y.(\lambda z.z[w/y])x
would be considered

\lambda y.(\lambda z.z)\Diamond
following (2) and
\lambda y.\Diamond
following (3).
Unfortunately, this definition is hard to specify
inductively (and therefore in
a big-steps semantics) without modifying the term first by permuting the cuts.
Interestingly though, giving a small-steps semantics for it simply amounts to
allowing
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
-reduction deep inside the distributors.

The Call-by-Need Strategy

The
call-by-need strategy

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
(
Figure 4
)
is defined on the set of terms
U
, by using
closure under the
need contexts
, given by the grammar

\mathtt{N}\Coloneqq\Diamond\mid\mathtt{N}t\mid\mathtt{N}[x\triangleleft t]\mid\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\mathtt{N}]

where
\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle\_\rangle\!\rangle
denotes capture-free application of contexts
(
Subsection 2.1
).
Like call-by-name (
Subsection 4.1
),
the call-by-need strategy is
weak
, because no
meaningful
reduction steps are performed under abstractions.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda x.p\rangle u&\mapsto_{\mathtt{dB}}&\mathtt{L}\langle p[x/u]\rangle\\
\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda y.p\rangle]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{spl}}&\mathtt{L}\langle\mathtt{LL}\langle\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.p^{\prime}]\rangle\rangle&\,\text{ if }\lambda y.{z[z/p]}\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle\\
\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{ls}}&\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]\end{array}

Figure 4.
Call-by-Need Strategy

Rule
\mathtt{dB}
is the same one used to define
\mathtt{name}
.
Rule
\mathtt{spl}
(named after splitting) only uses node replication operations to
compute the skeleton of the abstraction, while rule
\mathtt{ls}
implements
one-shot
linear
substitution.
There is no rule to substitute a variable, as it is usually done in call-by-need for closed terms
[
AF97
]
.

Linear substitution (replacing one free occurrence of a
variable at a time) as implemented by rule
\mathtt{ls}

above is not captured by the
calculus
\lambda R
. This shows a limitation of
\lambda R
and
\lambda a
,
both using full substitution to implement fully lazy sharing. Yet,
the demand-driven philosophy of call-by-need is generally understood
as replacing only some desired instance of one
variable
[
AF97
]
. This corresponds in particular to the
behavior of abstract machines, which make explicit some of the
implementation features. Nonetheless, remark that the substitution
used in the small-steps semantics
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
is linear, thanks to
the restriction on terms. Therefore, designing
\mathtt{flneed}
as a strategy
of a linear calculus with node replication is straightforward.

Notice that as a particular case of
14
,

t\in\texttt{U}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}t^{\prime}
implies
t^{\prime}\in\texttt{U}
.
Another interesting property is that
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}t^{\prime}
implies
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)\geq\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime})
. Moreover,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
is deterministic.

Lemma 22

(Determinism)
.

The strategy
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
is deterministic.

Proof 4.11
.

The left hand sides of the rules
\mathtt{dB}
,
\mathtt{dist}
and
\mathtt{ls}
are
disjoint. On the other hand, the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}

is confluent and terminating by
19
so that

\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
defines a function, thus the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
is deterministic.

{exa}

Let
t_{0}=(\lambda x.(\mathtt{I}(\mathtt{I}x)))(\lambda y.y\mathtt{I})
. Needed variable occurrences are highlighted in
orange
.

\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}t_{0}&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}(\underline{\mathtt{I}(\mathtt{I}x)})[x/\lambda y.y\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x_{1}$}[x_{1}/\underline{\mathtt{I}x}][x/\lambda y.y\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\underline{x_{1}[x_{1}/x_{2}[x_{2}/\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x$}]][x/\lambda y.y\mathtt{I}]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}\underline{x_{1}[x_{1}/x_{2}[x_{2}/\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x$}]][x/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}]}[z_{1}/\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}\underline{x_{1}[x_{1}/\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x_{2}$}[x_{2}/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}]]}[x/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}][z_{1}/\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}x_{1}[x_{1}/\underline{\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x_{2}$}[x_{2}/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{2}}]}[z_{2}/z_{1}]][x/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}][z_{1}/\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}\underline{\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x_{1}$}[x_{1}/(\lambda y.{yz_{2}})\hbox{\pagecolor{verdelight}$[x_{2}/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{2}}][z_{2}/z_{1}]$}]}[x/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}][z_{1}/\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}\underline{\hbox{\pagecolor{naranjalight}$x_{1}$}[x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{3}}]}[z_{3}/z_{2}][x_{2}/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{2}}][z_{2}/z_{1}][x/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}][z_{1}/\mathtt{I}]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}(\lambda y.{yz_{3}})[x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{3}}][z_{3}/z_{2}][x_{2}/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{2}}][z_{2}/z_{1}][x/\!\!/\lambda y.{yz_{1}}][z_{1}/\mathtt{I}]\end{array}

In order to characterize
\mathtt{flneed}
-nfs, we use
the notion of
needed free variables
, defined as:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{1cm}}l}\operatorname{ndv}(x):=\{x\}\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\quad\operatorname{ndv}(t[y/u]):=\begin{cases}(\operatorname{ndv}(t)\setminus\{y\})\cup\operatorname{ndv}(u)&\text{if }y\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)\\
\operatorname{ndv}(t)&\text{if }y\notin\operatorname{ndv}(t)\end{cases}\\
\operatorname{ndv}(tu):=\operatorname{ndv}(t)\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\quad\operatorname{ndv}(t[x/\!\!/u]):=\operatorname{ndv}(t)\\
\operatorname{ndv}(\lambda x.t):=\emptyset\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt\end{array}

Thus
e.g.

\operatorname{ndv}(x[y/\!\!/\mathtt{I}]{\mathtt{I}})=\{x\}

and
\operatorname{ndv}((xy_{1})[x/zy_{2}])=\{z\}
.
In particular,
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)
implies
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
.

{restatable}

lemndvprop
Let
t\in\texttt{U}
. Then
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)
iff there exists a context
\mathtt{N}

such that
t=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
.

Proof 4.12
.

By induction on
t
for the left-to-right implication and by induction on
\mathtt{N}

for the other one
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:ndv_prop).

Terms of
U
in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf can be characterized by the grammar
Ne
,
defined upon the grammar of neutral terms
\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
.
Notice that
\mathtt{name}
-nfs are also
\mathtt{flneed}
-nfs.

Ne

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda x.t\rangle\mid\overline{\texttt{Ne}}

\displaystyle\overline{\texttt{Ne}},\overline{\texttt{Ne}}_{0}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq x\mid\overline{\texttt{Ne}}\,t\mid\overline{\texttt{Ne}}[x\triangleleft u]\,\,x\notin\operatorname{ndv}(\overline{\texttt{Ne}})\mid\overline{\texttt{Ne}}[x/\overline{\texttt{Ne}}_{0}]\,\,x\in\operatorname{ndv}(\overline{\texttt{Ne}})

{restatable}

lemneednf
Let
t\in\texttt{U}
. Then
t\in\texttt{Ne}
iff
t
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf.

Proof 4.13
.

By induction on the grammars
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:need_nf).

5.
A Type System for
\lambda R

This section introduces a quantitative type system
\cap R

for
\lambda R
. Non-idempotent
intersection
[
Gar94
]
has one main advantage over the
idempotent model
[
BDS13
]
: it gives
quantitative

information about the length of reduction sequences to normal
forms
[
dC07
]
. Indeed, not only typability and normalization
can be proved to be equivalent, but a measure based on type
derivations provides an
upper bound
to normalizing reduction
sequences. This was extensively investigated in different
logical/computational
frameworks
[
AGL19
,
BKRV20
,
CG14
,
Ehr12
,
Kes16
,
KV20
]
. However,
no quantitative result based on types exists in the literature for the node replication model,
including the attempts done for deep inference
[
GHP21
]
.
The typing rules of our system are in
themselves not surprising (see for example
[
KV14
]
where a similar system is used for a
\lambda
-calculus with explicit substitutions interpreting the logical cut rule), but they provide
a handy quantitative characterization of fully lazy
normalization (
Section 6
).

Types are built on the following grammar of types and
(finite) multi-types, where
\alpha
ranges over a set of base types,

\mathtt{a}
is a special type constant used to type
terms reducing to normal abstractions and
I
ranges over
finite sets.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\mbox{({Types})}&\sigma&:=&\mathtt{a}\mid\alpha\mid\mathcal{M}\to\sigma\\
\mbox{({Multi-Types})}&\mathcal{M}&:=&[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\end{array}

We write
|\mathcal{M}|
to denote the
size of a multi-type

\mathcal{M}
.

Typing environments
, written
\Gamma
,
\Delta
,
\Sigma
are
functions from variables to multi-types, assigning the empty
multiset to all but a finite set of variables. The domain of

\Gamma
is given
by
\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma):=\{x\mid\Gamma(x)\neq[\,]\}
.
The
union of environments
, written
\Gamma\uplus\Delta
, is defined
by
(\Gamma\uplus\Delta)(x):=\Gamma(x)\sqcup\Delta(x)
, where

\sqcup
denotes multiset union. For instance,

(x:[\sigma],y:[\tau])\uplus(x:[\sigma],z:[\tau])=(x:[\sigma,\sigma],y:[\tau],z:[\tau])
. This
notion is extended to several environments as expected, so that

\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma_{i}
denotes a finite union of environments,
and the empty environment
when
I=\emptyset
. We write
\Gamma;\Delta
for
\Gamma\uplus\Delta
when

\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\operatorname{dom}(\Delta)=\emptyset
.

Type judgments
have the form
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
, where

\Gamma
is a typing environment,
t
is a term and
\sigma
is a type.

\frac{}{x:[\sigma]\vdash x:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({ax})}\frac{\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\to\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:\mathcal{M}\to\sigma\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash tu:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({app})}\frac{}{\emptyset\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({ans})}\frac{\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash t[x\triangleleft u]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}\!\frac{(\Gamma_{i}\vdash t:\sigma_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\uplus_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}\vdash t:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}}\quad\textsc{right=({many})}

Figure 5.
Typing System
\cap R

A
(typing) derivation
is a tree obtained by applying the
(inductive) typing rules of system
\cap R

(
Figure 5
), introduced
in
[
KV14
]
. The notation
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma

means there is a derivation named
\Phi
of the judgment

\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
in system
\cap R
. A term
t
is
typable in system
\cap R
, or
\cap R
-typable,
iff there is an environment
\Gamma
and a type
\sigma

such that
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
.
The
size of a type derivation

\operatorname{sz}({\Phi})

is defined as the number of its rules
(\textsc{abs})
,
(\textsc{app})
and
(\textsc{ans})
.
The typing system is
relevant
:

Lemma 23

(Relevance)
.

If
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
, then

\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma)\subseteq\operatorname{fv}(t)
.

Proof 5.1
.

Straightforward by induction on the typing derivation.

{exa}

The following tree is a type derivation (called
\Phi_{u}
) in system
\cap R

for the term
u=x[x/yz]
.

\frac{\frac{}{x:[\tau]\vdash x:\tau}\quad\textsc{Right=({ax})}\quad\frac{\frac{}{y:[[\tau]\to\tau]\vdash y:[\tau]\to\tau}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({ax})}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{}{z:[\tau]\vdash z:\tau}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=({ax})}}{z:[\tau]\vdash z:[\tau]}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\frac{y:[[\tau]\to\tau],z:[\tau]\vdash yz:\tau}{y:[[\tau]\to\tau],z:[\tau]\vdash yz:[\tau]}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({app})}}{y:[[\tau]\to\tau],z:[\tau]\vdash x[x/yz]:\tau}\quad\textsc{Right=({cut})}

Type derivations can be measured by triples.
We use a + operation on triples as pointwise addition:

(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})+(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})=(n_{1}+m_{1},n_{2}+m_{2},n_{3}+m_{3})
.
These triples are computed
by a
weighted derivation level
function
defined on typing derivations as
\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi\right):=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,1\right)
,
where
\mathsf{M}\left(-,-\right)
is inductively defined below.
In the cases
(\textsc{abs})
,
(\textsc{app})
and
(\textsc{cut})
, we let
\Phi_{t}
(resp.
\Phi_{u}
)
be the subderivation of the type of
t
(resp.
\Phi_{u}
)
and in
(\textsc{many})
we let
\Phi_{t}^{i}
be the
i
-th derivation of the type of
t
for each
{i\in I}
.

•

For (
ax
),
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{x},m\right)=(0,0,1)
,

•

For (
abs
),
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda x.t},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+(1,m,0)
.

•

For (
ans
),
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda x.t},m\right)=(1,m,0)
.

•

For (
app
),

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{tu},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(1,m,0)
.

•

For (
cut
),

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t[x\triangleleft u]},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])\right)
.

•

For (
many
),
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)=\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{i}_{t},m\right)
.

Intuitively, the first (resp. third) component of the

3
-tuple
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)
counts the number of application/abstraction
(resp. axiom) rules in the typing derivation and do not depend on
m
. The second one takes into account the
number of application/abstraction rules as well, but
weighted

by the level of the constructor.
The
3
-tuples are ordered lexicographically.

{exa}

Take the derivation
\Phi_{u}
from
Section 5
.
Its measure is
\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{u}\right)=(1,2,3)
. Moreover, for
x[x/yz]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}(x_{1}x_{2})[x_{1}/y][x_{2}/z]
we have
\Phi_{u^{\prime}}\rhd y:[\sigma],z:[\tau]\vdash(x_{1}x_{2})[x_{1}/y][x_{2}/z]:\tau

and
\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{u^{\prime}}\right)=(1,1,4)
.

{restatable}

lemmoreweight
For all derivation
\Phi
and all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m>n
,

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi}),0)
.

Proof 5.2
.

By induction on
\Phi
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:more_weight).

Lemma 24

(Split)
.

Let
\Phi\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M}=\sqcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{M}_{i}
for
I\neq\emptyset
.
Then there are derivations

\Phi_{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}_{i}
such that
\Delta=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{i}

and
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)=\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{i},m\right)
.

Proof 5.3
.

Straightforward.

6.
Observational Equivalence

The type system
\cap R
characterizes normalization of both

\mathtt{name}
and
\mathtt{flneed}
strategies as follows: every typable
term normalizes and every normalisable term is typable. In this
sense, system
\cap R
can be seen as a (quantitative)

model

[
BE01
]

of our call-by-name and call-by-need strategies. We
prove these results by studying the appropriate lemmas, notably
weighted subject reduction and weighted subject expansion. We then
deduce observational equivalence between the
\mathtt{name}
and the

\mathtt{flneed}
strategies from the fact that their associated normalization
properties are both fully characterized by the same typing system.

Soundness

Soundness of system
\cap R
w.r.t. both
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
and

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
is investigated in this section. More precisely, we show
that typable terms are normalizing for both strategies. In contrast
to reducibility techniques needed to show this kind of result for
simple types
[
GHP13b
]
, soundness is achieved here by relatively simple
combinatorial arguments based again on decreasing measures. We start
by studying the interaction between system
\cap R
and linear as well as
full substitution.

{restatable}

[Partial Substitution]lempartialsub
Let
\Phi\rhd\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma

and
\sqsubseteq
denote multiset inclusion. Then,
there exists
\mathcal{N}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{M}
such that for every
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{N}
we have
\Psi\rhd\Gamma\uplus\Delta;x:\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
and, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{M}\left(\Psi,m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{C})\right)-(0,0,|\mathcal{N}|)
.

Proof 6.1
.

By induction on
\Phi
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:partial-substitution).

Corollary 25

(Substitution)
.

If
\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma
and

\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
, then

\Phi\rhd\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash t\{x/u\}:\sigma
, and
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)
.
Moreover,
|\mathcal{M}|>0
iff the inequality is strict.

Proof 6.2
.

The proof is by induction on
|t|_{x}
.
If
|t|_{x}=0
, then by the relevance Lemma
23

\mathcal{M}=[\,]
,
so that
\Phi_{u}
necessarily comes from a
(\textsc{many})
rule without any premise and
thus
\Phi=\Phi_{t}
.
We have
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)
because

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)=(0,0,0)
.
Otherwise,
|t|_{x}>0
and we can write
t
as
\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
.
By the partial substitution
Section 6
,
there exists
\mathcal{N}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{M}
such that for all
\Phi_{u}^{0}\rhd\Delta_{0}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}
,
there is
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma\uplus\Delta_{0};x:\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
.
By the split
24
, there are derivations

\Phi_{u}^{1}\rhd\Delta_{1}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}
and

\Phi_{u}^{2}\rhd\Delta_{2}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{N}
,
where
\Delta=\Delta_{1}\uplus\Delta_{2}

so that we can apply the partial substitution Lemma to

\Phi_{t}
and
\Phi^{1}_{u}
, and we obtain

\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma\uplus\Delta_{1};x:\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
.
Since
\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{C})\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)
, we have

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{1},m+\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{C})\right)-(0,0,|\mathcal{N}|)\leq_{L.\ref{l:more_weight}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{1},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)-(0,0,|\mathcal{N}|)\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{1},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)
.
Because
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
,
|\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle|_{x}=|t|_{x}-1
.
We conclude by applying the i.h. on
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi_{u}^{2}
.
We get
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\uplus\Delta_{1}\uplus\Delta_{2}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle\{x/u\}:\sigma=\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash t\{x/u\}:\sigma
.
For the measure, we use
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle)\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)
to get

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{2},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle)\right)\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{1},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{2},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)=_{L.~\ref{l:split}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)
.
If
\mathcal{M}\neq[\,]
, then either
\mathcal{N}
or
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{N}
is non-empty,
so at least one of the two previous inequalities is strict.

The key idea to show soundness is that the measure
\mathsf{D}\left(\cdot\right)

decreases w.r.t. the reduction relations
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
:

{restatable}

[Weighted Subject Reduction for
\rightarrow_{\pi}
]lemsrpermlr
Let
\Phi_{t_{0}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{0}:\sigma
and
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\pi}t_{1}
.
Then there exists
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:\sigma
such that

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof 6.3
.

Let
t_{0}=\mathtt{C}\langle t_{0}^{\prime}\rangle
and
t_{1}=\mathtt{C}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle
,
where
t_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\pi}t_{1}^{\prime}
is a root step.
We reason by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.
We only detail one base case (where
\mathtt{C}=\Diamond
) and one inductive case
(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:sr_permlr).

•

t_{0}^{\prime}=t[x\triangleleft u[y\triangleleft s]]\mapsto_{\pi}t[x\triangleleft u][y\triangleleft s]=t_{1}^{\prime}
,
where
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
. Let
\Phi_{i}
be

\frac{\Phi_{u}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{u}^{i};y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash u:\rho_{i}\frac{\left(\Phi_{s}^{i,j}\rhd\Delta_{s}^{i,j}\vdash s:\delta_{j}\right)_{{j\in J_{i}}}}{\Delta_{s}^{i}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}_{i}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Delta_{u}^{i}\uplus\Delta_{s}^{i}\vdash u[y\triangleleft s]:\rho_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

then the typing derivation
\Phi
is of the form

\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\frac{\left(\Phi_{i}\rhd\Delta_{u}^{i}\uplus\Delta_{s}^{i}\vdash u[y\triangleleft s]:\rho_{i}\right)_{{i\in I}}}{\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash u[y\triangleleft s]:\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash t[x\triangleleft u[y\triangleleft s]]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\mathcal{M}=[\rho_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{i}=[\delta_{j}]_{{j\in J_{i}}}
,

\Delta_{u}=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{u}^{i}
,
\Delta_{s}^{i}=\uplus_{{j\in J_{i}}}\Delta_{s}^{i,j}
, and

\Delta_{s}=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{s}^{i}
.
Let
\Phi_{s}
be

\frac{\left(\Phi_{s}^{i,j}\rhd\Delta_{s}^{i,j}\vdash s:\delta_{j}\right)_{{j\in J_{i}},{i\in I}}}{\Delta_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}

We then construct the following derivation
\Psi
.

\frac{\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\frac{\left(\Phi_{u}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{u}^{i};y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash u:\rho_{i}\right)_{{i\in I}}}{\Delta_{u};y:\mathcal{N}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u};y:\mathcal{N}\vdash t[x\triangleleft u]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}\Phi_{s}\rhd\Delta_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash t[x\triangleleft u][y\triangleleft s]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\mathcal{N}=\sqcup_{{i\in I}}\mathcal{N}_{i}
, so that

\mathcal{N}=[\delta_{j}]_{{j\in J_{i}},{i\in I}}
. Moreover, because
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
, we have that

\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t[x\triangleleft u])=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])
if
y\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
, and

\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t[x\triangleleft u])=0
otherwise.
Now, we show that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i,j},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s])\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i,j},m+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t[x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s])\right)
.
If
y\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
, this is immediate.
Otherwise, by the Relevance
23
we
have
J_{i}=[\,]
for any
i
thus
s
is necessarily typed with rule
(\textsc{many})
and no premise,
so that both measures are equal to (0,0,0).
Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{{i\in I}}\sum_{{j\in J_{i}}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i,j},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s])\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{{i\in I}}\sum_{{j\in J_{i}}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i,j},m+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t[x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s])\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Psi,m\right)

•

If
\mathtt{C}=u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}]
, then
we have
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash u:\sigma

and
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle:\mathcal{M}
.
By the i.h. there is

\Psi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{M}
,
so
\Psi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle]:\sigma
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])\right)

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Psi^{\prime},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Psi,m\right)

Lemma 26

(Weighted Subject Reduction for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
)
.

Let
\Phi_{t_{0}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{0}:\sigma
.
If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
,
then there exists
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:\sigma

such that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)\geq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof 6.4
.

As remarked in
Subsection 2.2
,
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
implies

t_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\pi}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}t_{1}
. By
Section 6
,
weighted subject reduction holds for

t_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\pi}t^{\prime}
, so it is sufficient to show the statement for
the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}
. We reason by induction
on this relation.
We show the base cases for
\mapsto_{\mathtt{app}^{\prime}}
and
\mapsto_{\mathtt{dist}^{\prime}}
,
the cases
\mapsto_{\mathtt{abs}^{\prime}}
and

\mapsto_{\mathtt{var}^{\prime}}
are simply by the substitution

25
, and the inductive cases are straightforward by the i.h.

•

t_{0}={t[x/us]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]=t_{1}
, where
y
and
z
are fresh variables. Let

\Phi_{i}
be of the form

\frac{\Phi^{i}_{u}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}_{i}\to\rho_{i}\Phi^{i}_{s}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}_{i}}{\Gamma^{i}_{u}\uplus\Gamma^{i}_{s}\vdash us:\rho_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({app})}

then the typing derivation
\Phi_{t_{0}}
is of the form

\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\frac{\left(\Phi_{i}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{u}\uplus\Gamma^{i}_{s}\vdash us:\rho_{i}\right)_{{i\in I}}}{\Gamma_{u}\uplus\Gamma_{s}\vdash us:\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{u}\uplus\Gamma_{s}\vdash t{[x/us]}:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\mathcal{M}=[\rho_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
,

\Gamma_{u}=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma^{i}_{u}
and
\Gamma_{s}=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma^{i}_{s}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,0)\right)

Let us consider
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};y:\mathcal{N}_{u};z:\mathcal{N}_{s}\vdash t\{x/yz\}:\sigma
,
obtained by
25
from
\Phi_{t}
and

\Phi_{yz}\rhd y:\mathcal{N}_{u};z:\mathcal{N}_{s}\vdash yz:[\rho_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
,
where
\mathcal{N}_{u}=[\mathcal{N}_{i}\to\rho_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{s}=\sqcup_{{i\in I}}\mathcal{N}_{i}
. Let

\Phi_{u}=\frac{(\Phi^{i}_{u}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}_{i}\to\rho_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Gamma_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}_{u}}\quad\textsc{right=({cut}),vcenter}\quad\Phi_{s}=\frac{(\Phi^{i}_{s}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Gamma_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}_{s}}\quad\textsc{right=({cut}),vcenter}

We construct the following derivation
\Phi_{t_{1}}
with two applications
of rule
(\textsc{cut})
.

\frac{\frac{\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};z:\mathcal{N}_{s};y:\mathcal{N}_{u}\vdash t\{x/yz\}:\sigma\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{u}\rhd\Gamma_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}_{u}}{(\Gamma^{\prime};z:\mathcal{N}_{s})\uplus\Gamma_{u}\vdash t\{x/yz\}[y/u]:\sigma}\Phi_{s}\rhd\Gamma_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}_{s}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{u}\uplus\Gamma_{s}\vdash t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]:\sigma}

We consider two cases to conclude:

–

If
\mathcal{M}=[\,]
, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+1\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)=_{L.~\ref{l:qsub}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

–

If
\mathcal{M}\neq[\,]
, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+1\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+1\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+1\right)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime},m\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq_{L.~\ref{l:qsub}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{yz},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),2)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle<\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{s}^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),2)\right)

\displaystyle<\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right).

•

t_{0}={t[x/\lambda y.u]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]]=t_{1}
.
The typing derivation is of the form

\Phi_{t_{0}}=\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\Phi_{\lambda y.u}\rhd\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}\vdash\lambda y.u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}\vdash t{[x/\lambda y.u]}:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut}),vcenter}

where

\Phi_{\lambda y.u}=\frac{\left(\frac{\Phi^{i}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u};y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash u:\rho_{i}}{\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u}\vdash\lambda y.u:\mathcal{N}_{i}\to\rho_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}\right)_{{i\in I}}\left(\frac{}{\vdash\lambda y.u:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({ans})}\right)^{k}}{\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}\vdash\lambda y.u:\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{right=({many}),vcenter}

and
\mathcal{M}=[\mathcal{N}_{i}\to\rho_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\sqcup[\underbrace{\mathtt{a},\dots,\mathtt{a}}_{k}]
,
with
\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}=\uplus_{i\in I}\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+k*(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,0)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,0)\right)

We construct the following derivation

\Phi_{t_{1}}=\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\Phi_{\lambda}\rhd\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}\vdash\lambda y.z[z/u]:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}\vdash t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut}),vcenter}

where

\Phi_{\lambda}=\frac{\left(\frac{\Phi_{\lambda}^{i}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u};y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash z[z/u]:\rho_{i}}{\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u}\vdash\lambda y.z[z/u]:\mathcal{N}_{i}\to\rho_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({abs}),vcenter}\right)_{{i\in I}}\left(\frac{}{\vdash\lambda y.z[z/u]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({ans})}\right)^{k}}{\Gamma_{\lambda y.u}\vdash\lambda y.z[z/u]:\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{right=({many}),vcenter}

with
\Phi_{\lambda}^{i}
of the form

\frac{\frac{}{z:[\rho_{i}]\vdash z:\rho_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({ax})}\Phi^{i}\rhd\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u};y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash u:\rho_{i}}{\Gamma^{i}_{\lambda y.u};y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash z[z/u]:\rho_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+k*(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),0)

\displaystyle+\sum_{{i\in I}}\left(\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{i},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)+(0,0,1)+(1,m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),0)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

Lemma 27

(Weighted Subject Reduction for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}
)
.

Let
\Phi_{t_{0}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{0}:\sigma
.
If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t_{1}
,
then there exists
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:\sigma

such that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
for
every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof 6.5
.

We prove that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
by
showing in particular that it is the first component of the

3
-tuple that strictly decreases.

We reason by induction on the reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}
.

•

If
t_{0}=\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda x.t\rangle u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\mathtt{L}\langle t[x/u]\rangle=t_{1}
,
then we reason by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.
The inductive step follows from
Section 6
, so we only show the base case
\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
.
The typing derivation
\Phi_{t_{0}}
is of the form

\frac{\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma}{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\to\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}\Phi_{u}\rhd\Gamma_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{u}\vdash(\lambda x.t)u:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({app})}

and
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(2,2*m,0)
.

We construct the following derivation.

\Phi_{t_{1}}=\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\Phi_{u}\rhd\Gamma_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{u}\vdash t[x/u]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut}),vcenter}

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:more_weight}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(0,(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)

\displaystyle<\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

Notice that it is the first component of the first

3
-tuple that strictly
decreases by
2
.

•

If
t_{0}=tu\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}u=t_{1}
, where
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}
, then
the property trivially holds by the i.h.

•

If
t_{0}=t[x/u]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}[x/u]=t_{1}
, where
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}{\setminus\!\!\setminus}\,x\uplus\Delta
and

\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma

and
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)
.
By the i.h. we have

\Phi_{t^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t^{\prime}:\sigma

and
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)>_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}},m\right)
, where
in particular it is the first component of the first
3
-tuple
that strictly decreases. Derivation
\Phi_{t_{1}}
is then
obtained by rule
(\textsc{cut})
from
\Phi_{t^{\prime}}
and
\Phi_{u}
.
We can conclude since:

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t^{\prime})+1\right)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:more_weight}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(0,(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t^{\prime})+1)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)

\displaystyle<_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(0,(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:more_weight}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

Note that even when
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t^{\prime})>\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)
, the
inequation
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)<\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)
is determined by
the strict relation between the first components of the
3
-tuples,
that is, the unweighted number of abstraction and application rules.

Lemma 28
.

Let
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\tau
.
Then there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
I\neq\emptyset
and
[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}

such that
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}

and for any variable
z
there is a proof

\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus z:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle z\rangle\!\rangle:\tau
. In
particular, if
z
is fresh, then
\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus z:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}=\Gamma^{\prime};z:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
.

Proof 6.6
.

By induction on
\mathtt{N}
.

•

If
\mathtt{N}=\Diamond
, this is straightforward by taking
\Gamma^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}=[\tau]
.

•

If
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}t
or
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}[x\triangleleft t]
,
then there is a derivation

\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\tau^{\prime}
,
such that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Gamma_{2}
and
\tau^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}\to\tau

or
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
, respectively.
By the i.h.
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\uplus x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
, so that

\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{2}
.

•

If
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle[y/\mathtt{N}_{2}]
,
the derivation is as follows.

\frac{\Gamma_{1};y:[\rho_{j}]_{{j\in J}}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle:\tau\quad\frac{\left(\Gamma_{j}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\rho_{j}\right)_{{j\in J}}}{\uplus_{j\in J}\Gamma_{j}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:[\rho_{j}]_{{j\in J}}}\quad\textsc{right=({many})}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle[y/\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle]:\tau}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

Where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{2}=\uplus_{{j\in J}}\Gamma_{j}
.
By the i.h. on
\mathtt{N}_{1}
,
\Gamma_{1};y:[\rho_{j}]_{{j\in J}}=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus y:[\rho_{j}]_{{j\in J}^{\prime}}
for some

\emptyset\neq J^{\prime}\subseteq J
. Thus
J\neq\emptyset
.
By the i.h. on
\mathtt{N}_{2}
, for every
{j\in J}
we have

\Gamma_{j}=\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}\uplus x:[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I_{j}}
,
where
I_{j}\neq\emptyset
and a proof
\Phi_{j}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}_{j}\uplus z:[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I_{j}}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle\!\langle z\rangle\!\rangle:\rho_{j}
for a
variable
z
.
We then take
I=\cup_{{j\in J}}I_{j}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}\uplus_{{j\in J}}\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 29

(Weighted Subject Reduction for
\mathtt{flneed}
)
.

Let
\Phi_{t_{0}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{0}:\sigma
.
If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}t_{1}
, then there exists

\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:\sigma

such that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
for
every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof 6.7
.

We prove that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
by
showing in particular that the first component of the
first
3
-tuple strictly decreases when the reduction
is
\mathtt{dB}
. We reason by induction on the reduction relation, i.e. by induction
on the context
\mathtt{N}
where the root reduction takes place.
We first detail the base case when
\mathtt{N}=\Diamond
.

•

t_{0}=\mathtt{L}\langle\lambda x.t\rangle u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}\mathtt{L}\langle t[x/u]\rangle=t_{1}
.
This case is the same as for
\mathtt{name}
.

•

t_{0}=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\lambda y.p]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}\mathtt{LL}\langle\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.p^{\prime}]\rangle=t_{1}
,
where
\lambda y.z[z/p]\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle
.
The typing derivation
\Phi_{t_{0}}
is of the
form

\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\frac{(\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.p:\sigma_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Delta\vdash\lambda y.p:\mathcal{N}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\lambda y.p]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\mathcal{N}=[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
,
\Delta=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{i}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta
. Moreover,
I\neq\emptyset
by
28
. For each
\sigma_{i}
we build the following derivations
\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i}
:

–

if
\sigma_{i}=\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\tau_{i}
then

\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i}
is of the form

\frac{\frac{}{z:[\tau_{i}]\vdash z:\tau_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({ax})}\quad\Phi_{p}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{i};y:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash p:\tau_{i}}{\frac{\Delta_{i};y:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash z[z/p]:\tau_{i}}{\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.z[z/p]:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\tau_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({cut})}

where
\Phi_{p}^{i}
is obtained from
\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i}
by reversing the
(\textsc{abs})
rule.

–

if
\sigma_{i}=\mathtt{a}
then

\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i}=\frac{}{\vdash\lambda y.z[z/p]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({ans}),vcenter}
.

By hypothesis,
\lambda y.z[z/p]\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle
.
Since
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
is included in
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
, then
we know
by
26
that there are derivations

\Phi_{p_{1}}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle:\sigma_{i}

such that
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{i}_{p_{0}},m\right)\geq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{i}_{p_{1}},m\right)
.
Thus, we can build the following derivation.

\Phi^{\prime}_{t_{1}}=\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\frac{(\Phi_{p_{1}}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle:\sigma_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Delta\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{N}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

Let
n=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)
.
We begin showing that

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i},m+n+1\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i},m+n\right)
for every
i\in I
.
There are two cases.

(1)

If
\sigma_{i}=\mathtt{a}
, then

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i},m+n+1\right)=(1,m+n+1,0)
,
while
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i},m+n\right)=(1,m+n,0)
.

(2)

If
\sigma_{i}=\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\tau_{i}
, then

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i},m+n+1\right)=(1,m+n+1,0)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p}^{i},m+n+1\right)
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i},m+n\right)

\displaystyle=(1,m+n,0)+(0,0,1)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p}^{i},m+n+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(z)+1\right)

\displaystyle=(1,m+n,1)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p}^{i},m+n+1\right).

So that

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i},m+n+1\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i},m+n\right)
since
(1,m+n+1,0)>(1,m+n,1)
.

Finally, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime}_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p_{1}}^{i},m+n\right)

\displaystyle\leq_{L.~\ref{l:sr_nrsub}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{p_{0}}^{i},m+n\right)

\displaystyle<\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\sum_{{i\in I}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{\lambda y.p}^{i},m+n+1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

By
Section 6
, we can finally construct

\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{LL}\langle\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.p^{\prime}]\rangle:\sigma
, where
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi^{\prime}_{t_{1}},m\right)
.

•

t_{0}=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]=t_{1}
.
The typing derivation
\Phi_{t_{0}}
is of the form

\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\quad\frac{(\Phi^{i}_{v}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash v:\tau_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Phi_{v}\rhd\Delta\vdash v:\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\mathcal{M}=[\tau_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
and
\Delta=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{i}
.
By
28
we know that there is a non-empty
\mathcal{N}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{M}
which types the variable
x

in the hole of the context
\mathtt{N}
.
We can then write
\mathcal{M}
as
\mathcal{N}\sqcup\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
By
24
there are two derivations
\Phi_{v_{1}}\rhd\Delta_{1}\vdash v:\mathcal{N}
and
\Phi_{v_{2}}\rhd\Delta_{2}\vdash v:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
such that
\Delta=\Delta_{1}\uplus\Delta_{2}

and
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{1}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{2}},m\right)
. Using

Section 6
, we can construct:

\Phi_{t_{1}}=\frac{\Psi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{1};x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\quad\Phi_{v_{2}}\rhd\Delta_{2}\vdash v:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta;x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut}),vcenter}

We clearly have
\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{N})\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)

and, because
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(v)
,
we also have
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle)\leq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Psi,m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{2}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle)\right)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:partial-substitution}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{1}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{\Diamond}(\mathtt{N})\right)-(0,0,|\mathcal{N}|)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{2}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{1}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)\right)-(0,0,|\mathcal{N}|)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{2}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)\right)

\displaystyle<\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{1}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{v_{2}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

Now, we analyse all the inductive cases of the form

t_{0}=\mathtt{N}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}\mathtt{N}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle=t_{1}
,
where
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}t^{\prime}_{1}
.

(1)

If
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}u
, then we have

\Phi_{t_{0}^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t_{0}^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{N}\to\sigma

and
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{N}
.
By the i.h. there is
\Phi_{t_{1}^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{N}\to\sigma
,
so
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle u:\sigma
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}^{\prime}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(1,m,0)>_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}^{\prime}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(1,m,0)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
.

(2)

If
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]
, then we have

\Phi_{t_{0}^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t_{0}^{\prime}\rangle:\sigma

and
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
.
By the i.h. there is

\Phi_{t_{1}^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle:\sigma
,
so
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle[x\triangleleft u]:\sigma
. We distinguish three different cases:

•

If
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}t^{\prime}_{1}
is a
\mathtt{dB}
-step: then we know by the i.h. that

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}^{\prime}},m\right)>\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}^{\prime}},m\right)
strictly decreases
the first component of the first
3
-tuple. We then have

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{1}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)+1\right)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:more_weight}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{1}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(0,(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)+1)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)

\displaystyle<_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{0}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(0,(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle)+1)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)

\displaystyle=_{L.~\ref{l:more_weight}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{0}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle)+1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

•

If
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}t^{\prime}_{1}
is an
\mathtt{spl}
-step, then

t^{\prime}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t^{\prime}_{1}
, so that

\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle
,
and thus

\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle)\geq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)

holds by
Subsection 2.3
. We then conclude by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{1}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)+1\right)

\displaystyle<_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{0}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{0}},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle)+1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)

•

If
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}t^{\prime}_{1}
is a
\mathtt{ls}
-step, then we
know that
\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle
also holds, then
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle)\geq\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)
.
We conclude as before.

(3)

If
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\mathtt{N}_{2}]
, then
we have
\Phi_{1}\rhd\Delta;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma

and
\Phi_{t_{0}^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle t_{0}^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{M}
.
By the i.h. there is

\Phi_{t_{1}^{\prime}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{M}
,
so
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x\triangleleft\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle]:\sigma
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}},m\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{1},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{0}^{\prime}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle t_{0}^{\prime}\rangle])\right)>_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{1},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}^{\prime}},m+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle t_{1}^{\prime}\rangle])\right)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t_{1}},m\right)
.

{exa}

Consider the following reduction sequence:

(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.zy][z/\mathtt{I}]

We have
\Phi_{1}\rhd\emptyset\vdash(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y]:\mathtt{a}

with
\Phi_{1}
of the form

\frac{\frac{\Phi_{\mathtt{I}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}}}{x_{1}:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}):\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({app})}\quad\frac{\Phi_{\mathtt{I}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{}{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash y:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=({ax})}}{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash y:[\mathtt{a}]}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\frac{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash\mathtt{I}y:\mathtt{a}}{\frac{\emptyset\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y:[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}}{\emptyset\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({abs})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({app})}}{\emptyset\vdash(\mathtt{I}(x_{1}\mathtt{I}))[x_{1}/\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{Right=({cut})}

where

\Phi_{\mathtt{I}}=\frac{\frac{}{x:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash x:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{Right=({ax})}}{\emptyset\vdash\mathtt{I}:[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({abs}),vcenter}

and

\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}}=\frac{\frac{}{x_{1}\vdash[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]:x_{1}{[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}}}\quad\textsc{right=({ax})}\quad\frac{\frac{}{\emptyset\vdash\mathtt{I}:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({ans})}}{\emptyset\vdash\mathtt{I}:[\mathtt{a}]}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\frac{x_{1}:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{1}\mathtt{I}:\mathtt{a}}{x_{1}:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{1}\mathtt{I}:[\mathtt{a}]}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({app}),vcenter}

We also have
\Phi_{2}\rhd\emptyset\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y]:\mathtt{a}
with
\Phi_{2}
of the form
