\frac{\frac{\frac{}{x_{2}:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{2}:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=({ax})}\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}}}{x_{1}:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}\quad\frac{\Phi_{\mathtt{I}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{}{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash y:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=({ax})}}{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash y:[\mathtt{a}]}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\frac{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash\mathtt{I}y:\mathtt{a}}{\frac{\emptyset\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y:[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}}{\emptyset\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({abs})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({app})}}{\emptyset\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{Right=({cut})}

Concerning the measures we have

\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{1}\right)=(7,10,4)>(5,13,4)=\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{2}\right)
.
The first element of the
3
-tuple decreases from
7
to
5
because
we lost an abstraction and an application constructors
during
\mathtt{dB}
-reduction. Note also that in
\Phi_{1}
we
have
\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}},1\right)=(2,2,1)
while in
\Phi_{2}
we have

\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}},2\right)=(2,4,1)=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}},1\right)+(0,\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}}}),0)
.
Besides, we have
\Phi_{3}\rhd\emptyset\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.zy][z/\mathtt{I}]:\mathtt{a}
where
\Phi_{3}
is of the form

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{x_{2}:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{2}:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right=({ax})}\Phi_{x_{1}\mathtt{I}}}{x_{1}:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}]:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=({cut})}\Phi^{\prime}_{3}}{z:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.zy]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}\Phi_{\mathtt{I}}}{\emptyset\vdash x_{2}[x_{2}/x_{1}\mathtt{I}][x_{1}/\!\!/\lambda y.zy][z/\mathtt{I}]:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{Right=({cut})}

where
\Phi^{\prime}_{3}
is

\frac{\frac{}{z:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash z:[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({ax})}\frac{\frac{}{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash y:\mathtt{a}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({ax})}}{y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash y:[\mathtt{a}]}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\frac{z:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}];y:[\mathtt{a}]\vdash zy:\mathtt{a}}{\frac{z:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash\lambda y.zy:[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}}{z:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]\vdash\lambda y.zy:[[\mathtt{a}]\to\mathtt{a}]}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=({many})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({abs})}}\quad\textsc{Right=({app})}

Therefore
\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{3}\right)=(5,11,5)<(5,13,4)=\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{2}\right)
,
where the second element of the
3
-tuple
has decreased from
13
to
11
because
two nodes of the term
\lambda y.\mathtt{I}y
, namely the binder and
the application, have moved from the explicit substitution of level
3

to the distributor of level

2
.

Theorem 30

(Typability implies
\mathtt{name}
-Normalization)
.

Let
\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
.
Then
t
is
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing.
Moreover, the first element of
\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{t}\right)
is an upper bound for
the number of
\mathtt{dB}
-steps to
\mathtt{name}
-nf.

Proof 6.8
.

Suppose
t
is not
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing.
Since
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
is terminating by
5
,
then every infinite
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
-reduction sequence starting at
t

must necessarily have an infinite number of
\mathtt{dB}
-steps.
Moreover, all terms in such an infinite sequence are typed
by
27
and

26
.
Therefore, these lemmas
guarantee that all

\mathtt{dB}
/
\mathtt{sub}
reduction steps involved in such

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
-reduction sequence do not increase the measure
\mathsf{D}\left(\cdot\right)
, and that, in particular,
\mathtt{dB}
-steps strictly decrease it by
decreasing the first element of the triple.
This leads to a contradiction because the order
>
on 3-tuples
\mathsf{D}\left(\cdot\right)
is well-founded. Then
t
is necessarily
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing.

Theorem 31

(Typability implies
\mathtt{flneed}
-Normalization)
.

Let
\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
.
Then
t
is
\mathtt{flneed}
-normalizing. Moreover, the first element of

\mathsf{D}\left(\Phi_{t}\right)
is an upper bound for the number of
\mathtt{dB}
-steps to
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf.

Proof 6.9
.

The property trivially holds
by
29
since the lexicographic order
on
3
-tuples is well-founded.

Completeness

We address here completeness of system
\cap R
with respect to

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{flneed}}
. More precisely, we show that
normalizing terms in each strategy are typable. The
basic property in showing that
consists in guaranteeing that normal forms are typable.

{restatable}

[
\mathtt{flneed}
-nfs are Typable]lemndlrnormtyp
Let
t
be in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf.
Then there exists a derivation
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau

such that for any
x\notin\operatorname{ndv}(t)
,
\Gamma(x)=[\,]
.

Proof 6.10
.

By
Subsection 4.2
we can reason by induction on the grammar
Ne

(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:ndlr-norm-typ).

{exa}

Remember that
\operatorname{ndv}((xy_{1})[x/z]{y_{1}})=\{z\}
and note that
\operatorname{ndv}(xy_{1})=\{x\}
.

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{x:[\,]\to\tau\vdash x:[\,]\to\tau}\frac{}{\emptyset\vdash y_{1}:[\,]}}{x:[\,]\to\tau\vdash xy_{1}:\tau}\frac{\frac{}{z:[\,]\to[\,]\to\tau\vdash z:[\,]\to[\,]\to\tau}\frac{}{\emptyset\vdash y_{2}:[\,]}}{z:[\,]\to[\,]\to\tau\vdash zy_{2}:[\,]\to\tau}}{z:[\,]\to[\,]\to\tau\vdash(xy_{1})[x/zy_{2}]:\tau}

Because
\mathtt{name}
-nfs are also
\mathtt{flneed}
-nfs,
we infer the following corollary for free.

Corollary 32

(
\mathtt{name}
-nfs are Typable)
.

Let
t
be in
\mathtt{name}
-nf. Then there is a derivation
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
.

We need lemmas stating the behavior of partial and full (anti-)substitution
w.r.t. typing.

{restatable}

[Partial Anti-Substitution]lempartialasub
Let
\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
and
u
be terms s.t.
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
and
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
.
Then
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\exists\Delta
,
\exists\mathcal{M}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime}
,
\exists\Phi_{u}

s.t.
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta
,
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus x:\mathcal{M}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
and
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
.

Proof 6.11
.

By induction on
\mathtt{C}

(see appendix on
\autopageref
p:partial-anti-substitution).

Corollary 33

(Anti-Substitution)
.

Let
u
be a term s.t.
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
and

\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t\{x/u\}:\sigma
. Then
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
,

\exists\Delta
,
\exists\mathcal{M}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime}
,
\exists\Phi_{u}

s.t.
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta
,
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma

and
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
.

Proof 6.12
.

The proof is by induction on
|t|_{x}
.

•

If
|t|_{x}=0
then
t\{x/u\}=t
and, by

23
,
x\notin\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma)
then
\Gamma=\Gamma;x:[\,]
. Therefore, for
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma
,
\Delta:=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{M}=[\,]
,
\Phi^{\prime}:=\Phi
and
\Phi_{u}:=\frac{}{\vdash u:[\,]}
the result holds.

•

If
|t|_{x}\geq 1
then let
\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
such that
t\{x/u\}=\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle u\rangle\!\rangle
.
For any fresh
y
, we have that
t\{x/u\}=\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle\{y/u\}

where
\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle=t^{\prime}\{x/u\}
s.t.
t=t^{\prime}\{y/x\}
. Note that

|t^{\prime}|_{x}<|t|_{x}
. Then by

Section 6

\exists\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
,
\exists\Delta^{\prime}
,
\exists\mathcal{N}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime\prime}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime}_{u}
s.t.
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus y:\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
and
\Phi^{\prime}_{u}\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}
where, by freshness of
y
,
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus y:\mathcal{N}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime};y:\mathcal{N}
. Therefore, by the i.h. on
\Phi^{\prime\prime}

\exists\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}
,

\exists\Delta^{\prime\prime}
,
\exists\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{u}

s.t.
\Gamma^{\prime\prime};y:\mathcal{N}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime};x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}:\sigma

and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{u}\rhd\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. By freshness of
y
and
relevance,
we have
y\notin\operatorname{dom}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})
.
Then
\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}=\Gamma^{iv};y:\mathcal{N}

where
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}=\Gamma^{iv}\uplus\Delta^{\prime\prime}
. From
\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}
and

Section 6
we have
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd(\Gamma^{iv};x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime})\uplus x:\mathcal{N}\vdash t:\sigma
while from
\Phi^{\prime}_{u}
and

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{u}
we obtain
\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}\sqcup\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Finally, for
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma^{iv}
,
\Delta:=\Delta^{\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}\sqcup\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
the result holds, since
(\Gamma^{iv};x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime})\uplus x:\mathcal{N}=\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta=\Gamma^{iv}\uplus\Delta^{\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime}=\Gamma
.

To achieve completeness, we show that typing is preserved by anti-reduction.

Lemma 34

(Subject Expansion)
.

Let
\Phi_{t_{1}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{1}:\sigma
.
If
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{r}}t_{1}
, where
\mathtt{r}\in\{\pi,\mathtt{sub},\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB},\mathtt{flneed}\}
,
then there exists
\Phi_{t_{0}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{0}:\sigma
.

Proof 6.13
.

The proof is by induction on
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{r}}
and uses

Section 6
and
33
.
We detail some interesting cases of the proof.
In all the cases shown, we suppose that the list context
\mathtt{L}
of the
general rule is empty (
\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
), since we can use subject expansion for

\rightarrow_{\pi}
to manipulate it.

•

t_{0}=t[x/us]\mapsto_{\mathtt{sub}}t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]=t_{1}
.
Then
\Phi_{t_{1}}
is of the form

\frac{\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};z:\mathcal{N}_{s};y:\mathcal{N}_{u}\vdash t\{x/yz\}:\sigma\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{u}\rhd\Delta_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{N}_{u}}{(\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u});z:\mathcal{N}_{s}\vdash t\{x/yz\}[y/u]:\sigma}\quad\Phi_{s}\rhd\Delta_{s}\vdash s:\mathcal{N}_{s}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]:\sigma}

where
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}
. Also
(\Gamma^{\prime};z:\mathcal{N}_{s})\uplus\Delta_{u}=(\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u});z:\mathcal{N}_{s}
since
z\notin\operatorname{dom}(\Delta_{u})
by the Relevance
23
.
By
33

\exists\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
,

\exists\Delta
,
\exists\mathcal{M}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime}
,
\exists\Phi_{yz}
s.t.

\Gamma^{\prime};z:\mathcal{N}_{s};y:\mathcal{N}_{u}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta
,
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma
and
\Phi_{yz}\rhd\Delta\vdash yz:\mathcal{M}
. By freshness of
y,z
and

23
we have that
y,z\notin\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})\cup\{x\}
.
Then
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Delta=z:\mathcal{N}_{s};y:\mathcal{N}_{u}
. From
\Phi_{yz}
,

\Phi_{u}
,
\Phi_{s}
and
Section 6
we obtain
\Phi_{us}\rhd\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash us:\mathcal{M}
and construct
\Phi_{t_{0}}
as:

\Phi_{t_{0}}=\frac{\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\quad\Phi_{us}\rhd\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash us:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta_{u}\uplus\Delta_{s}\vdash t[x/us]:\sigma}

•

If
t_{0}=(\lambda x.t)u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dB}}t[x/u]=t_{1}
.
Then
\Phi_{t_{1}}
is of the form

\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\quad\Phi_{u}\rhd\Gamma_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{u}\vdash t[x/u]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

Therefore, we construct
\Phi_{t_{0}}
as follows:

\frac{\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma}{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\to\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}\quad\Phi_{u}\rhd\Gamma_{u}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Gamma_{u}\vdash(\lambda x.t)u:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({app})}

•

t_{0}=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\lambda y.p]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}\mathtt{LL}\langle\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.p^{\prime}]\rangle=t_{1}
,
where
\lambda y.z[z/p]\Downarrow_{\mathtt{st}}\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle
.
By subject expansion for
\rightarrow_{\pi}
, there is
\Phi_{t^{\prime}_{1}}\rhd\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle]:\sigma
and
it is of the form

\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\quad\frac{(\Phi_{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle:\sigma_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Delta\vdash\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle:\mathcal{N}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/\lambda y.\mathtt{LL}\langle p^{\prime}\rangle]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\Delta=\uplus_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{i}
and
\mathcal{N}=[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
where, by
28
,
\mathcal{N}\neq[\,]
.
Then, for each
{i\in I}
we have by subject expansion for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}

(of which
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{st}}
is a subrelation) that
\Phi^{\prime}_{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.z[z/p]:\sigma_{i}
which has
two different shapes, depending on
\sigma_{i}
.

(1)

If
\sigma_{i}=\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\tau_{i}
then
\Phi^{\prime}_{i}
is of the form

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{z:[\tau_{i}]\vdash z:\tau_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=({ax})}\hskip 8.19447pt\Phi_{p}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{i};y:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash p:\tau_{i}}{\Delta_{i};y:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash z[z/p]:\tau_{i}}\quad\textsc{Right=({cut})}}{\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.z[z/p]:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\tau_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}

Therefore we have
\Psi_{i}
of the form

\frac{\Phi_{p}^{i}\rhd\Delta_{i};y:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash p:\tau_{i}}{\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.p:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\tau_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({abs})}

(2)

If
\sigma_{i}=\mathtt{a}
then
\Delta_{i}=\emptyset
and we obtain
\Psi_{i}
of the form

\frac{}{\vdash\lambda y.p:\mathtt{a}}\quad\textsc{right=({ans}),vcenter}
.

We can then construct
\Phi_{t_{0}}
as follows

\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\frac{(\Psi_{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash\lambda y.p:\sigma_{i})_{{i\in I}}}{\Delta\vdash\lambda y.p:\mathcal{N}}\quad\textsc{Right=({many})}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\lambda y.p]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

•

t_{0}=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]=t_{1}
. Then
\Phi_{t_{1}}
is of the form

\frac{\Phi\rhd\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\quad\Phi^{\prime}_{v}\rhd\Delta^{\prime}\vdash v:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}{\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

By
Section 6

\exists\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
,

\exists\Delta^{\prime\prime}
,
\exists\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime}
,
\exists\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{v}

s.t.
\Gamma^{\prime};x:\mathcal{N}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{v}\rhd\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vdash v:\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}
. From
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(v)
and the Relevance
23
we
have that
x\notin\operatorname{dom}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})
. Thus
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime};x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
and then

\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\uplus x:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime};x:\mathcal{N}
where
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\sqcup\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}
. From
\Phi^{\prime}_{v}

and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{v}
derivations we obtain
\Phi_{v}\rhd\Delta\vdash v:\mathcal{N}
, where
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime\prime}
. Then
\Phi_{t_{0}}
is of the form

\frac{\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime};x:\mathcal{N}\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle:\sigma\Phi_{v}\rhd\Delta\vdash v:\mathcal{N}}{\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta\vdash\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle[x/\!\!/v]:\sigma}\quad\textsc{right=({cut})}

where
\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\uplus\Delta=\Gamma^{\prime}\uplus\Delta^{\prime}
.

Theorem 35

(
\mathtt{name}
-Normalization implies Typability)
.

Let
t
be a term. If
t
is
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing,
then
t
is
\cap R
-typable.

Proof 6.14
.

Let
t
be
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing.
Then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}^{n}u

and
u
is a
\mathtt{name}
-nf. We reason by induction on
n
.
If
n=0
, then
t=u
is typable by
32
.
Otherwise, we have
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{name}}^{n-1}u
.
By the i.h.
t^{\prime}
is typable and thus by

34
(because
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{sub}}
is included in
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}
),

t
turns out to be also typable.

Theorem 36

(
\mathtt{flneed}
-Normalization implies Typability)
.

Let
t
be a term. If
t
is
\mathtt{flneed}
-normalizing, then

t
is
\cap R
-typable.

Proof 6.15
.

Similar to the previous proof but using
Section 6

instead of
32
.

Summing up, Theorems
30
,
35
,
31
and
36
give:

Theorem 37
.

t\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
is
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing
iff
t
is
\mathtt{flneed}
-normalizing
iff
t
is
\cap R
-typable.

All the technical tools are now available to
conclude observational equivalence between our two
evaluation strategies based on node replication.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be any reduction
notion on
\mathtt{T}_{R}
. Then, two terms
t,u\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
are said to
be
\mathcal{R}
-observationally equivalent
, written
t\equiv_{\mathcal{R}}u
, if for any context
\mathtt{C}
,
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
is
\mathcal{R}
-normalizing

iff

\mathtt{C}\langle u\rangle
is
\mathcal{R}
-normalizing.

Theorem 38
.

For all terms
t,u\in\mathtt{T}_{R}
,
t
and
u

are
\mathtt{name}
-observationally equivalent iff
t
and
u

are
\mathtt{flneed}
-observationally equivalent.

Proof 6.16
.

The proof uses
37
.
Indeed, we have
t\equiv_{\mathtt{name}}u

iff (
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
is
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing
iff
\mathtt{C}\langle u\rangle
is
\mathtt{name}
-normalizing for any context
\mathtt{C}
)
iff
(
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
is
\mathtt{flneed}
-normalizing
iff
\mathtt{C}\langle u\rangle
is
\mathtt{flneed}
-normalizing for any context
\mathtt{C}
)
iff
t\equiv_{\mathtt{flneed}}u
.

7.
Related Works and Conclusion

Several calculi with explicit substitutions (ES) bridge the gap between formal higher-order calculi and
concrete implementations of programming languages (see a survey
in
[
Kes07
]
). The first of such calculi,

e.g.

[
ACCL90
,
BR95
]
, were all based on
structural

substitution, in the sense that the ES operator is syntactically
propagated step-by-step through the term structure until a variable is reached, when the
substitution finally takes place. The correspondence between ES and
Linear Logic Proof-Nets
[
DCKP03
]
led to the more recent notion of
calculi
at a
distance

[
AK10
,
ABKL14
,
Acc18a
]
,
enlightening a natural and new application of the Curry-Howard
interpretation. These calculi implement linear/partial substitution

at a distance
, where the search of variable occurrences is abstracted
out with context-based rewriting rules, and thus no ES propagation
rules are necessary. A third model was introduced by the seminal work
of Gundersen, Heijltjes, and Parigot
[
GHP13a
,
GHP13b
]
,
introducing the atomic
\lambda
-calculus to implement node replication.

Inspired by the last approach we introduced the calculus
\lambda R
,
capturing the essence of node replication. In contrast
to
[
GHP13a
]
, we work with an implicit (structural)
mechanism of weakening and contraction, a design choice which aims at
focusing and highlighting the node replication model, which is the
core of our calculus, so that we obtain a rather simple and natural
formalism used in particular to specify evaluation strategies. Indeed,
besides the proof of the main operational meta-level properties of our
calculus (confluence, termination of the substitution calculus,
simulations), we use linear and non-linear versions of
\lambda R
to
specify evaluation strategies based on node replication, namely
call-by-name and call-by-need evaluation strategies. In
particular, we provided simple tools to prove correctness of these
reduction strategies. This was achieved in the framework of our
concise calculus
\lambda R
, based not only on an implicit
treatment of weakening and contraction, but also on the notion of
commuting conversions by means of
distance
. Indeed, the
treatment of weakening, contraction, and commuting conversions
result in a heavy machinery for the atomic
\lambda
-calculus, which
would make the correctness of these strategies much more involved.

Moreover, characterisation of termination of different strategies based on
\lambda R
were achieved with a rather standard type system and, surprisingly, no deep inference system was necessary at this point. This is of interest, since our type system is equipping full-laziness with a well-known denotational semantics. We think that this would be difficult to achieve in the framework of the atomic
\lambda
-calculus.

The first description of call-by-need was given by
Wadsworth
[
Wad71
]
, where reduction is performed on

graphs
instead of terms. Weak call-by-need on
terms
was
then introduced by Ariola and Felleisen
[
AF97
]
, and by
Maraist, Odersky and Wadler
[
MOW98
,
MOTW99
]
. Reformulations
were introduced by Accattoli, Barenbaum and Mazza

[
ABM14
]
and by Chang and Felleisen
[
CF12
]
. Our
call-by-need strategy is inspired by the calculus
in
[
ABM14
]
, which uses the distance
paradigm
[
AK10
]
to gather together meaningful
and permutation rules, by clearly separating

multiplicative
from
exponential
rules, in the sense of
Linear Logic
[
Gir87
]
.

Full laziness has been formalized in different ways. Pointer
graphs
[
Wad71
,
SW10
]
are DAGs allowing for an elegant
representation of sharing. Labeled calculi
[
Lév78
,
BLM05
]
implement
pointer graphs by adding annotations to
\lambda
-terms, which makes
the syntax more difficult to handle.
Lambda-lifting
[
Hug83
,
Jon87
]
implements full laziness
by resorting to translations from
\lambda
-terms to supercombinators.
In contrast to all the previous formalisms, our calculus is defined on
standard
\lambda
-terms with explicit cuts, without the use of any
complementary syntactical tool. So is Ariola and Felleisen’s
call-by-need
[
AF97
]
, however, their notion of full laziness
relies on external (ad-hoc) meta-level operations used to extract the
skeleton. Our specification of call-by-need enables fully lazy
sharing, where the skeleton extraction operation is internally encoded
in the term calculus operational semantics. Last but not least, our
calculus has strong links with proof-theory, notably deep inference.

Balabonski
[
Bal12b
,
Bal12a
]
relates many
formalisms of full laziness and shows that they are equivalent when
considering the number of
\beta
-steps to a normal form. It would
then be interesting to
understand if his unified approach,
(abstractly) stated by means of the theory of
residuals
[
Lév78
,
Lév80
]
, applies to our own strategy.

We have also studied the calculus from a semantical point of view, by
means of intersection types. Indeed, the type system can be seen as a
model of our implementations of call-by-name and call-by-need, in the
sense that typability and normalization turn out to be equivalent.

Intersection types go back to
[
CD78
]
and
have been
used to provide characterizations of qualitative
[
BDS13
]

as well as quantitative
[
dC07
]
models of the

\lambda
-calculus, where typability and
normalization coincide. Quantitative models
specified by means of non-idempotent
types
[
Gar94
,
Kfo00
]
were first applied to
the
\lambda
-calculus (see a survey in
[
BKV17
]
) and to several other
formalisms ever since, such as
call-by-value
[
Ehr12
,
CG14
]
,
call-by-need
[
Kes16
,
AGL19
]
,
call-by-push-value
[
GM18
,
BKRV20
]
and classical
logic
[
KV20
]
. In the present work, we achieve for the first time a quantitative
characterization of fully lazy normalization, which provides upper
bounds for the length of reduction sequences to normal forms.

Characterizations provided by intersection type systems
sometimes lead to observational
equivalence results (
e.g.

[
Kes16
]
).
In this work we succeed to
prove observational equivalence related to a fully lazy implementation
of weak call-by-need, a result which would be extremely involved
to prove by means of syntactical tools of rewriting,
as done for weak call-by-need in
[
AF97
]
.
Moreover, our result implies that our node replication implementation
of full laziness is observationally equivalent to standard call-by-name
and to weak call-by-need (see
[
Kes16
]
), as well as to
the more semantical notion of neededness (see
[
KRV18
]
).

A Curry-Howard interpretation of the logical
switch
rule of
deep inference is given in
[
She19
,
SHGP20
]
as an
end-of-scope operator, thus introducing the
spinal atomic

\lambda
-calculus
. The calculus implements a refined optimization of
call-by-need, where only the
spine
of the abstraction (tighter
than the skeleton) is duplicated. It would be interesting to adapt

\lambda R
to spine duplication by means of an appropriate end-of-scope operator,
such as the one in
[
HvO03
]
. Further optimizations might also be
considered.

Finally, this paper only considers weak evaluation strategies,
i.e.
with
reductions forbidden
under abstractions, but it would be interesting to extend our notions
to full (strong) evaluations too
[
GL02
,
BBBK17
]
.
Extending full laziness to classical logic would be another
interesting research direction, possibly taking preliminary ideas
from
[
He18
]
. We
would also like to investigate (quantitative)
tight
types for
our fully lazy strategy, as done for weak call-by-need
in
[
AGL19
]
, which does not seem evident in our node
replication framework.

Appendix A
Proofs

\levelsubstitution

*

Proof A.1
.

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
, then
t\{x/p\}=t
and the property holds in both
cases since
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)=0
.
Let
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
. We show the two cases.

(1)

z\notin\operatorname{fv}(p)
. We reason by induction on
t
.

•

If
t=x
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{z}(x\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(p)=0=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(x)
.

•

If
t=\lambda y.t^{\prime}
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{z}((\lambda y.t^{\prime})\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(\lambda y.t^{\prime}\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\})=_{\textit{i.h.}}\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(\lambda y.t^{\prime})
.

•

If
t=t_{1}t_{2}
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{z}((t_{1}t_{2})\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}t_{2}\{x/p\})
.
Then,

\operatorname{lv}_{z}((t_{1}t_{2})\{x/p\})=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\}))

=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}))=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}t_{2})
.

•

If
t=t^{\prime}[y\triangleleft u]
, then
t\{x/p\}=t^{\prime}\{x/p\}[y\triangleleft u\{x/p\}]
.
There are two cases.

–

If
z\notin\operatorname{fv}(u\{x/p\})
, that is
z\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\}[y\triangleleft u\{x/p\}])=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\})=_{\textit{i.h.}}\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}[y\triangleleft u])
.

–

Otherwise,

\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\}[y\triangleleft u\{x/p\}])=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(u\{x/p\})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft u]))=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t^{\prime})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft u]))=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}[y\triangleleft u])
.

(2)

z\in\operatorname{fv}(p)
. By induction on
t
.

•

If
t=x
, then

t\{x/p\}=p
and

\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(p)=0=\max(0,0)=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(x),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(x)).

•

If
t=\lambda y.t^{\prime}
, then

t\{x/p\}=\lambda y.t^{\prime}\{x/p\}
and

\operatorname{lv}_{z}(\lambda y.t^{\prime}\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}\{x/p\})=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t^{\prime}))=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(\lambda y.t^{\prime}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\lambda y.t^{\prime})).

•

If
t=t_{1}t_{2}
then
t\{x/p\}=t_{1}\{x/p\}t_{2}\{x/p\}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, one has either
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i}\{x/p\})=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{i}))
by i.h. if
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{i})
, or
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i}\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i})
by
Point
1
otherwise.

–

If
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{1})\cap\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\}))

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1})),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2}))

\displaystyle=\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}t_{2}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}t_{2}))

–

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\}))

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}+P.\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1})),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}))

\displaystyle=\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),0))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}t_{2}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}t_{2}))

–

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{1})
the case is as above.

•

If
t=t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]
then

t\{x/p\}=t_{1}\{x/p\}[y\triangleleft t_{2}\{x/p\}]
.
By
\alpha
-conversion we can assume

y\notin\operatorname{fv}(p)
. By i.h.  one has
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i}\{x/p\})=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{i}))
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
if
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{i})
,
\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i}\{x/p\})=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{i})
otherwise.
There are two cases.

(a)

If
z\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2}\{x/p\})
then
z\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
and
necessarily
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
since
z\in\operatorname{fv}(p)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

(b)

If
z\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{2}\{x/p\})
then,

(i)

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{1})
, then
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=_{P.\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2}))+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}])),\\
\phantom{(\max(\max}\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))&z\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})\\
\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))&z\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

(ii)

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
, then
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{1})
and
z\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}+P.\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}])),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

(iii)

If
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{1})\cap\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/p\})

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}\{x/p\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}\{x/p\})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1})),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2}))+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]),

\displaystyle\phantom{=\max(}\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]),\\
\phantom{\max(}\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))&z\in\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})\\
\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{2})+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft t_{2}]))&z\notin\operatorname{fv}(t_{2})\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t_{1}[y\triangleleft t_{2}]))

\stabilitylevels

*

Proof A.2
.

(1)

Let
t_{0}=\mathtt{C}\langle o\rangle
and
t_{1}=\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle
, where
o\rightarrow_{\pi}o^{\prime}
is a root step.
We reason by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.
First we consider the base cases, where
\mathtt{C}=\Diamond
.

•

t_{0}=\lambda y.t[x\triangleleft u]\rightarrow_{\pi}(\lambda y.t)[x\triangleleft u]=t_{1}
, where
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
.
We have two cases:

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda y.t[x\triangleleft u])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x\triangleleft u])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda y.t)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}((\lambda y.t)[x\triangleleft u])

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda y.t[x\triangleleft u])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x\triangleleft u])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda y.t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\lambda y.t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}((\lambda y.t)[x\triangleleft u])

•

t_{0}=t[x\triangleleft u]s\rightarrow_{\pi}(ts)[x\triangleleft u]=t_{1}
, where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(s)
.
We have two cases:

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}({t[x\triangleleft u]}s)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}({t[x\triangleleft u]}),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(ts)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}({(ts)[x\triangleleft u]})

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}({t[x\triangleleft u]}s)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}({t[x\triangleleft u]}),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),0)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle(x\notin\operatorname{fv}(s))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(ts),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(ts)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}({(ts)[x\triangleleft u]})

•

t_{0}=ts[x\triangleleft u]\rightarrow_{\pi}(ts)[x\triangleleft u]=t_{1}
, where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
.
We have two cases:

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(ts[x\triangleleft u])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s[x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(ts)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}({(ts)[x\triangleleft u]})

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(ts[x\triangleleft u])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s[x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(ts),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(ts)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}((ts)[x\triangleleft u])

•

Let
t_{0}=t[y\triangleleft s[x\triangleleft u]]\rightarrow_{\pi}t[y\triangleleft s][x\triangleleft u]=t_{1}
, where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
.
We have four cases:

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(s)\cup\operatorname{fv}(u)
:

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s[x\triangleleft u]])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s][x\triangleleft u])

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(s),w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s[x\triangleleft u]])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s[x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s][x\triangleleft u])

(c)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(s),w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s[x\triangleleft u]])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s[x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))

\displaystyle\geq\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s]),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t[y\triangleleft s])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s][x\triangleleft u])

(d)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(s)\cap\operatorname{fv}(u)
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s[x\triangleleft u]])\\
&=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s[x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))\\
&=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))\\
&=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]),\\
&\qquad\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))\\
&=\max(\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s])),\\
&\qquad\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+\operatorname{ES}([y\triangleleft s]))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))\\
&\geq\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s]),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t[y\triangleleft s])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))\\
&=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[y\triangleleft s][x\triangleleft u])\end{array}

The inductive cases are the following:

•

If
\mathtt{C}=\lambda x.\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
, where
x\neq w
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda x.\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}({\mathtt{C}^{\prime}}\langle o\rangle)\geq_{\textit{i.h.}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}({\mathtt{C}^{\prime}}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

•

If
\mathtt{C}=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}u
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle u)=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u))\geq_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u))=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

•

If
\mathtt{C}=u\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle))\geq_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle))=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

•

If
\mathtt{C}=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]
, then

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle[x\triangleleft u])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)\geq_{\textit{i.h.}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle[x\triangleleft u])=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))\geq_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u]))=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle[x\triangleleft u])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

•

If
\mathtt{C}=u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}]
, then

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle])=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle]))\geq_{\textit{i.h.}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o\rangle]))=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u[x\triangleleft\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{C}\langle o^{\prime}\rangle)
.

(2)

We reason by induction on the reduction
relation, i.e. by induction on the context
\mathtt{C}
where
the root reduction takes place.
We detail the base case which is
\mathtt{C}=\Diamond
.
In all such cases we use Point
1

to push
\mathtt{L}
outside, i.e. we can write
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}}t_{1}
as

t_{0}\rightarrow_{\pi}\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle=t_{1}
,
where
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}t^{\prime}_{1}
does not push any list context outside.
We then show the property for steps
t^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{sub}^{\prime}}t^{\prime}_{1}

not pushing any substitution outside
and we conclude by

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t_{0})\geq_{P.\ref{l:stability-levels-permutation}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{0}\rangle)\geq\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{L}\langle t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t_{1})
.
The inductive cases for
\mathtt{C}
are treated as in Point
1
.

•

t^{\prime}_{0}={t[x/us]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{app}}{t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s]}=t^{\prime}_{1}
, where
y
and
z
are fresh variables.

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(us)
(i.e.
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
and
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(s)
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
and
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(s)
. There are two cases.

(i)

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(us)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),0+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

(ii)

If
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(us)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+0+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+0)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(yz))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

(c)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
and
w\in\operatorname{fv}(s)
. There are two cases.

(i)

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(us)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),0+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u]),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

(ii)

If
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(us)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+0+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+0)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(yz))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u]),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

(d)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
and
w\in\operatorname{fv}(s)
. There are two cases.

(i)

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(us)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s))+1))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u]),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

(ii)

If
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/us])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(us)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+0)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\max(\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{z}(yz))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+0)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\max(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(yz))+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1,\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u]),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\})+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u]),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u])+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(s)+1)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/yz\}[y/u][z/s])

•

t^{\prime}_{0}={t[x/\lambda y.u]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{dist}}{t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]]}=t^{\prime}_{1}
.
There are two cases.

(a)

w\notin\operatorname{fv}(\lambda y.u)
:

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/\lambda y.u])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]])

(b)

w\in\operatorname{fv}(\lambda y.u)
(i.e.
w\in\operatorname{fv}(u)
and
w\neq y
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/\lambda y.u])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda y.u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\max(0,0+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(z),\operatorname{lv}_{z}(z)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(u)+1))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(z[z/u]))

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\lambda y.z[z/u]))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.z[z/u]])

•

t^{\prime}_{0}={t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{abs}}t\{x/\lambda y.u\}=t^{\prime}_{1}
,
where
u
is pure. There are two cases:

(a)

If
w\notin\operatorname{fv}(\lambda y.u)
or
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}.\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}({t\{x/\lambda y.u^{\prime}\}})=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(\mathtt{L}\langle t\{x/\lambda y.u^{\prime}\}\rangle)

(b)

If
w\in\operatorname{fv}(\lambda y.u)
and
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/\!\!/\lambda y.u])=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}.\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/\lambda y.u\})

•

t^{\prime}_{0}={t[x/y]}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{var}}{t\{x/y\}}=t^{\prime}_{1}
.

(a)

If
w\neq y
:

\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/y])=\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}.\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/y\})

(b)

If
w=y
and
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/y])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(y)+1)

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),1)

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t)

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}.\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/y\})

(c)

If
w=y
and
x\in\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t[x/y])

\displaystyle=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{w}(y)+1)

\displaystyle\geq\max(\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t))

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}.\ref{l:level-substitution-non-void}}\operatorname{lv}_{w}(t\{x/y\})

\mssubstitution

*

Proof A.3
.

By induction on
t
.
In this proof,
\level(o)
denotes the first element of an object

o\in\mathcal{O}
:
\level(\subesname(k,n))=k
and
\level(\subdistname(k))=k
.
If
\subesname(k,n)\in\mathcal{O}
we also define
\snd(\subesname(k,n))=n
.

•

If
t=y
, then
\mathsf{CL}\left(y\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(y\{x/p\}\right)=[\,]
so
the property is straightforward for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

If
t=\lambda y.u
, then

\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(u\{x/p\}\right)

and
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u)
.
The property trivially holds by the i.h.

•

If
t=u_{1}u_{2}
, then
we have
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)

and
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1}u_{2})=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{2}))
.
Let
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)
thus

o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)
.
Suppose w.l.o.g. that
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)
.
Let
K_{1}=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1})\leq K
. By the i.h. we have either
(1)
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)_{\subdistname}
,
(2)
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)^{>K_{1}}_{\subesname}
, or
(3)
o=\subesname(k,n)
where
k\leq K_{1}
and
n\leq N_{1}

for some
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
.
If (1) holds, then
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}
and we are done. Otherwise,

o=\subesname(k,n)
, and we consider two cases.

–

If
k>K
, then (2) implies

o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
which implies

o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
while
(3) implies
k\leq K
which leads to a contradiction.

–

If
k\leq K
, we are done.

Considering that
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)
we have
a similar result for some
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
. We thus have the
result for
N=\max(N_{1},N_{2})
.

•

If
t=u_{1}[y/u_{2}]
, then
we can assume by
\alpha
-conversion that
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(p)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1,|u_{2}|)]\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1}\{x/p\})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)

\displaystyle\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1}\{x/p\})+1,|u_{2}\{x/p\}|)]

\displaystyle=_{L.\ref{l:level-substitution}:\ref{l:level-substitution-void}}\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1,|u_{2}\{x/p\}|)]

There are two cases:

(1)

If
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(u_{2})
, then

\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1})
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)
and
|u_{2}\{x/p\}|=|u_{2}|
. Let
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)
.

–

If
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)
, then let
K_{1}=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1})=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)=K
, so that the i.h. gives either (1)
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)_{\subdistname}
, (2)
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)^{>K_{1}}_{\subesname}
, or (3)
o=\subesname(k,n)
where
k\leq K_{1}
and
n\leq N_{1}
for some
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
. If (1) holds, then
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}
and we are
done. If (2) holds, then
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
since

K_{1}=K
, which implies
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
and we
are done. Otherwise, (3) holds and
k\leq K_{1}=K
as
required.

–

If
o\in(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)=(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)
, then
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{\leq K}_{\subesname}
, which particularly implies in the
last case that
o=\subesname(k,n)
and
k\leq K
. Note that, since
\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{\leq K}_{\subesname}
is finite, we can take
N_{2}=\max\{\snd(o)\;|\;o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{\leq K}_{\subesname}\}
.

–

If
o=\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1,|u_{2}\{x/p\}|)=\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1,|u_{2}|)
, then
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)
and thus either
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}
or
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{\leq K}
, which particularly implies in the
last case that
\level(o)\leq K
and

\snd(o)\leq N_{2}
, with
N_{2}
defined as above.

The result then holds for
N=\max(N_{1},N_{2})
.

(2)

If
x\in\operatorname{fv}(u_{2})
, then
\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)=\max(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1}),\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{2})+1)
. Let
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\{x/p\}\right)
.

–

If
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\{x/p\}\right)
, then let
K_{1}=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{1})=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)\leq K
, so that the i.h. gives either (1)

o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)_{\subdistname}
, (2)
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)^{>K_{1}}_{\subesname}
, or (3)
o=\subesname(k,n)
where
k\leq K_{1}
and
n\leq N_{1}
for some
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
. If (1) holds, then
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}
and we
are done. Otherwise
o=\subesname(k,n)
and we consider
two cases.

*

If
k>K
, then (2) implies

o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{1}\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
, and thus
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
,
while (3) implies
k\leq K

which leads to a contradiction.

*

If
k\leq K
, then we are done.

–

If
o\in(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)
, then there is
o^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\{x/p\}\right)
such that

\level(o)=\level(o^{\prime})+(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)
. Let
K_{2}=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(u_{2})
, so that the i.h. gives either (1)
o^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)_{\subdistname}
, (2)
o^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)^{>K_{2}}_{\subesname}
,
or (3)
o^{\prime}=\subesname(k,n)
where
k\leq K_{2}
and

n\leq N_{2}
for some
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
. If (1)
holds, then
o\in(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)_{\subdistname}
, thus
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)_{\subdistname}
and we are done.
If (2) holds, then
o\in(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u_{2}\right)^{>K_{2}}_{\subesname}
and thus
\level(o)>K_{2}+(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1)
. We consider two cases.

*

If
\level(o)>K\geq K_{2}+\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1
,
then (2) implies
o\in\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)^{>K}_{\subesname}
while (3) leads
to a contradiction.

*

If
\level(o)\leq K
, then we are done.

–

If
o=\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1,|u_{2}\{x/p\}|)
, then

\level(o)=\operatorname{lv}_{y}(u_{1})+1\leq K
and
\snd(o)=|u_{2}\{x/p\}|
.

The result then holds for
N=\max(N_{1},N_{2},|u_{2}\{x/p\}|)
.

•

If
t=u_{1}[y/\!\!/u_{2}]
, the analysis is similar.

\mspi

*

Proof A.4
.

Let
t=\mathtt{C}\langle t_{0}\rangle\rightarrow_{\pi}\mathtt{C}\langle t_{1}\rangle=t^{\prime}
,
where
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\pi}t_{1}
is a reduction step at the root
position. We proceed by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.
We detail the base case where the context
\mathtt{C}
is
\Diamond
,
by inspecting the cases where the explicit cuts are explicit substitutions,
as the remaining cases for explicit distributors are
similar.

(1)

If
t=t_{0}=\lambda y.t[x/u]\rightarrow_{\pi}(\lambda y.t)[x/u]=t_{1}=t^{\prime}
,
where
y\notin\operatorname{fv}(u)
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t[x/u]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(\lambda y.t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\lambda y.t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(\lambda y.t)+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)

(2)

If
t=t_{0}=t[x/u]s\rightarrow_{\pi}(ts)[x/u]=t_{1}=t^{\prime}
,
where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(s)
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t[x/u]\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(ts\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(ts)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(ts)+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)

(3)

If
t=t_{0}=ts[x/u]\rightarrow_{\pi}(ts)[x/u]=t_{1}=t^{\prime}
,
where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(s[x/u]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(ts\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(ts)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(ts)+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)

(4)

If
t=t_{0}=t[y/s[x/u]]\rightarrow_{\pi}t[y/s][x/u]=t_{1}=t^{\prime}
,
where
x\notin\operatorname{fv}(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s[x/u]\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1,|s[x/u]|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1)\cdot\left(\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+1,|u|)]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1,|s[x/u]|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+2)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+2,|u|),\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1,|s[x/u]|)]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1,|s[x/u]|)]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+2)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+2,|u|)]

\displaystyle>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}\left(\mathsf{CL}\left(t\right)\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(s\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+1,|s|)]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t[y/s])+1)\cdot\mathsf{CL}\left(u\right)\sqcup[\subesname(\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t[y/s])+1,|u|)]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{CL}\left(t^{\prime}\right)

The
>^{\mathcal{O}}_{\texttt{MUL}}{}
inequation is justified by the following facts:

•

|s[x/u]|>|s|
.

•

\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+2=\max(0,\operatorname{lv}_{y}(t)+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(s)+1)+1=\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t[y/s])+1
.

The inductive cases are straightforward.

⋄*

Proof A.5
.

We split the statement above in three different properties, each one proved by induction
on the involved relation relations.

(1)

If

, then there exists
t^{\prime}
such that

and
s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}t^{\prime}
.
We consider the following cases:

•

((

), (

))

such that

and

, where
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}u_{0}
and
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{dB}}s_{0}
.

and

.
Therefore

.

\ndvprop

*

Proof A.6
.

(1)

x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)
. By induction on
t
.

•

t=x
. Then we take
\mathtt{N}=\Diamond
.

•

t=t^{\prime}u
. By the i.h. there exists
\mathtt{N}^{\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
.
We then take
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}u
.

•

t=t^{\prime}[y/u]
.
By
\alpha
-conversion we can assume
x\neq y
. Either
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
or (
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(u)
and
y\in\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
).
In the first case, there exists by the i.h. on
t^{\prime}
a context
\mathtt{N}^{\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
.
We then take
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}[y/u]
.
In the second case, there exists by the i.h. on
t^{\prime}
a context
\mathtt{N}_{1}
such that
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle
.
By the i.h. on
u
we have
u=\mathtt{N}_{2}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
.
We then take
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle[y/\mathtt{N}_{2}]
.

•

t=t^{\prime}[x/\!\!/u]
.
By the i.h. there exists
\mathtt{N}^{\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
.
We then take
\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}[x/\!\!/u]
.

(2)

t=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
. By induction on
\mathtt{N}
.

•

\mathtt{N}=\Diamond
. Then
t=x
and
\operatorname{ndv}(t)=\{x\}
.

•

\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}u
. Then

t=t^{\prime}u
and by the i.h.
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
, so
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)
by definition.

•

\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]
. Then

t=t^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]
and by the i.h.
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
, so
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)
by definition.

•

\mathtt{N}=\mathtt{N}_{1}\langle\!\langle y\rangle\!\rangle[y/\mathtt{N}_{2}]
.
Then
t=t^{\prime}[y/u]
, where
y\in\operatorname{fv}(t^{\prime})
.
By the i.h.
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(u)
, so
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t)
.

\neednf

*

Proof A.7
.

We first show that
t\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
iff
t
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and
t
is not an answer.

\Rightarrow
:

We reason by induction on
t\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
.

•:

t=x
. This case is straightforward.

•:

t=t^{\prime}u
where
t^{\prime}\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
.
By the i.h.
t^{\prime}
is
in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and is not an answer,
so it is not possible to apply any
\mathtt{dB}
-reduction at the root. Then

t
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf, and since it is an application it is not
an answer.

•:

t=t^{\prime}[x\triangleleft u]
where
t^{\prime}\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
and
x\notin\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
. By the i.h.
t^{\prime}
is in

\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and it is not an answer. Moreover, we
cannot apply rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}
nor
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}
because by

Subsection 4.2
there is no context
\mathtt{N}

surrounding
x
. Then
t
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and is not an answer.

•:

t=t^{\prime}[x/u]
where
t^{\prime},u\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
and
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
.
By the i.h.
t^{\prime}
and
u
are in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and are not answers.
We cannot apply rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}
because

u
is not an answer. Then
t
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and is not an answer.

\Leftarrow
:

We reason by induction on
t
.

•:

t=x
is immediate.

•:

t=t^{\prime}u
. Then
t^{\prime}
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and is not an answer (otherwise
\mathtt{dB}
would be applicable).
By the i.h.
t^{\prime}\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
and thus
t\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
.

•:

t=t^{\prime}[x/u]
. Then
t^{\prime}
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf and is not an answer.
By the i.h.
t^{\prime}\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
. There are two cases.
If
x\notin\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
, then
t\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
by
definition and we are done.
Otherwise
x\in\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})
, and we get
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{N}\langle\!\langle x\rangle\!\rangle
by

Subsection 4.2
. Thus

u
cannot be an answer because
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{spl}}
would apply.
Moreveor,
u
is in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf because
otherwise
t
would not be in
\mathtt{flneed}
-nf.
Thus,
u\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
by the i.h.and
we get
t\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
by definition.

•:

t=t^{\prime}[x/\!\!/u]
.
We have
x\notin\operatorname{ndv}(t^{\prime})

\rightarrow_{\mathtt{ls}}
does not apply. By
the i.h.
t^{\prime}\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
, so that
t\in\overline{\texttt{Ne}}
.

Neutral terms are also normal.
Answers are normal because the calculus is weak and they belong to the grammar
Ne

Proof A.8

\Phi
.

•

If
\Phi=\frac{\Phi_{t}\rhd\Gamma;y:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda y.t:\mathcal{M}\to\tau}
, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+(1,m,0)

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{t}}),0)+(1,n,0)+(0,m-n,0)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{t}}),0)+(0,m-n,0)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,n\right)+(0,(m-n)*(\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{t}})+1),0)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi}),0)

,

, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},m\right)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},m\right)+(1,m,0)

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{t}}),0)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)+(1,n,0)+(0,m-n,0)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi,n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi}),0)

\displaystyle=_{\textit{i.h.}}\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{t},n\right)+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{t}}),0)+\mathsf{M}\left(\Phi_{u},n+\operatorname{lv}_{x}(t)+\operatorname{ES}([x\triangleleft u])\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt+(0,(m-n)*\operatorname{sz}({\Phi_{u}}),0)

Proof A.9
.

\Phi
.

If

24
gives

.

,

,
